<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

U.S. crude oil tops $100 as Iran escalates Hormuz attacks
At least nine commercial ships have come under attack near Hormuz since Sunday as Iran tries to choke off crude oil exports through the strait.
The "energy ceasefire" that President Donald Trump tried to broker between Ukraine and Russia has quickly unraveled.
Iran allied Houthi militants in Yemen have repeatedly fired missiles and drones at Saudi Arabia this week.
Rapidan Energy’s McNally: Three conflicts are impacting energy flows and fixed energy infrastructure
Crude oil prices rose sharply Friday as Iran escalated attacks in the Strait of Hormuz, Houthi militants fired missiles at Saudi Arabia and Ukraine vowed to keep striking Russian refineries.
Brent futures hit a session high of $110 per barrel for the first time since May before easing slightly to close at $109.86, a gain of 3.7%.
U.S. West Texas Intermediate rose 3.5% to settle at $101.41 per barrel. WTI added more than 10% this week while Brent advanced nearly 5%.
Saudi Arabia intercepted several missiles fired over the Red Sea on Friday, including one that was heading toward the oil export terminal Yanbu, a military spokesman said. Three drones were also destroyed in the kingdom&#x27;s Riyadh and southern regions, the military said.
Iran-allied Houthi militants in Yemen have repeatedly fired at Saudi Arabia this week. Oil giant Aramco came under attack when one of its administrative buildings in Dhahran was hit Tuesday, the kingdom&#x27;s energy ministry said .
The Houthis attacked Riyadh&#x27;s international airport on Oct. 10, killing 12 and injuring more than 300.
In the Strait of Hormuz, at least nine tankers and cargo ships have come under attack since Sunday as Iran tries to choke off oil exports through the waterway. Iran sank the container ship GFS Pride on Wednesday, U.S. Central Command said. The crew of the Singapore-registered ship was safely evacuated, according to Singapore&#x27;s maritime authority.
The attacks have left three seafarers injured and two missing this week, according to the International Maritime Organization .
Crude exports through Hormuz have pulled back after touching prewar levels in September. Shipments stood at 9.4 million barrels per day for the week ended Wednesday, about 70% of the average before the Iran war, according to data provided by the energy researcher Kpler.
Meanwhile, the " energy ceasefire " that President Donald Trump tried to broker between Ukraine and Russia has quickly unraveled. The Kremlin continued attacking Ukrainian infrastructure despite the supposed truce. President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said Kyiv would resume strikes on Russian refineries in retaliation.
Diesel prices have surged this year due to Ukraine&#x27;s attacks on Russian refineries and Iranian strikes on energy infrastructure in the Middle East. The fuel cost an average of $6.33 a gallon in the U.S. Friday, more than 70% above the same period last year, according to AAA data.
U.S. crude oil prices were also driven higher by a big decline of more than 8 million barrels for the week ended Oct. 9 in commercial inventories due to production shut-ins on the Gulf Coast from Hurricane Isaias, according to the Energy Information Administration.

### 3. ★★★★☆ Fed's Warsh withholds rate signal, praises recent global tightening

- **分类**: 💰 全球金融
- **来源**: CNBC Top
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T12:36:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Warsh refrained from signaling another interest rate hike this month, pointed to a "synchronous tightening" by central banks in the fight against inflation.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Fed&#x27;s Warsh withholds rate signal, praises recent global tightening
Fed Chair Kevin Warsh, in his speech Friday in Bangkok, declined to offer clues on whether there will be an interest rate hike again this month.
He acknowledged a "synchronous tightening" by central banks around the world, saying there are "no free riders."
He expressed optimism that artificial intelligence will make economies that use it well more productive and wealthier, warning the road there will be “very bumpy.”
Fed’s Warsh in Bangkok: &#x27;Not in that business anymore&#x27; of broadcasting decisions
BANGKOK — Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh refrained from signaling whether there will be another interest rate hike again this month, while acknowledging a "synchronous tightening" by central banks around the world.
"I don&#x27;t prejudge meetings. I don&#x27;t believe in saying now what I might do at the meetings that we have in the balance of this year," Warsh said, reiterating his opposition to the use of forward guidance for markets that should rely on interpreting data.
He also acknowledged that stance may disappoint anyone who had hoped for a steer ahead of the central bank meeting later this month. "It&#x27;s a new chapter at the Fed [and] we&#x27;re not in that business anymore," he said.
Warsh spoke at a fireside chat with the International Monetary Fund&#x27;s managing director, Kristalina Georgieva, as part of the weeklong IMF-World Bank Annual Meetings in Bangkok on Friday.
"Alongside other recent comments from FOMC participants, Warsh&#x27;s comments indicate that the FOMC is set to pause in October," Goldman Sachs economists led by Jan Hatzius wrote in a note. "We continue to expect a second and final hike in December."
S&P 500 and Nasdaq futures edged higher following the speech, while stock market indexes in Asia and Treasury yields were largely stable.
Chair of the Federal Reserve of the United States Kevin Warsh speaks during the International Monetary Fund (IMF) and World Bank Group annual meetings at the Queen Sirikit National Convention Center (QSNCC) in Bangkok, Thailand, on October 16, 2026.
Warsh described the global shift toward tighter monetary policy as a common fight on prices. Central banks made their own choices under their own mandates, he said, but the result was "a synchronous tightening in monetary policy in most places around the world," which means "there&#x27;s no free riders."
Surging energy costs stemming from the Iran war and artificial intelligence investment boom are stoking inflation and pushing up borrowing costs around the world, just as global public debt is on pace to exceed 100% of the world&#x27;s GDP, a level last breached in the aftermath of World War II.
The U.S. government has insisted that strong growth would be the best way to grow out of its rising debt pile, dismissing IMF&#x27;s warning that the plans of relying solely on growth won&#x27;t work as something that needs to be taken "with a grain of salt."
Warsh also expressed optimism about how AI will make economies that use it well more productive and wealthier, while warning the road there may be "very bumpy," and its near-term effect on employment is still uncertain.
"I want you to hear from me optimism, but optimism leavened by the seriousness of purpose," Warsh said.
A decade from now, he said, people will look back on the developments and "say this was hugely consequential."
The Fed raised rates to a range of 3.75% to 4% last month, its first hike since 2023 . Traders see a 72.3% chance for the Fed to keep the interest rate unchanged at the Oct. 27-28 meeting, according to CME FedWatch, while the majority expect a 25 basis point hike at the December meeting.

Iran says oil tanker hit naval mine in Strait of Hormuz
Iran says oil tanker hit naval mine in Strait of Hormuz
Revolutionary Guard Navy says supertanker carrying over 2M barrels of crude catches fire after explosion
HAMILTON, Canada Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) Navy said Friday that an oil supertanker struck a naval mine in the Strait of Hormuz, triggering a massive explosion and fire.
The vessel was carrying more than 2 million barrels of crude oil when it attempted to transit the strategic waterway, according to a statement carried by the semi-official Tasnim News Agency.
The IRGC Navy described the vessel as a "rogue" tanker that had switched off its navigation and radar systems.
It claimed the tanker had attempted to exploit US military support to transport goods through the strait.
The statement did not identify the vessel, its owner or flag.
It also provided no information about possible casualties or the extent of the damage.
This came amid heightened tensions between Iran and the US over freedom of navigation through the Strait of Hormuz, a major passage for global oil and gas shipments.
Maritime traffic through the waterway has been severely disrupted since the US and Israel launched attacks against Iran on Feb. 28.

The Houthis on Thursday launched a strike against the presidential palace in government-controlled Yemen. The attack caused multiple death and injuries among the security staff, according to the internationally recognized government .
The Saudi-led coalition battling the Iran-backed Houthis in Yemen said Thursday it had intercepted a cruise missile over Saudi Arabia's western Medina region. The Houthis called the claim " a shameless and brazen lie ."
Iran president says conflict in Mideast amounts to "ecocide"
Iranian President Masoud Pezeshkian on Friday urged countries to condemn what he called "ecocide" in the Middle East caused by "wars of aggression" against his country.
Pezeshkian said the conflicts in Gaza and the wider Middle East had taken a significant toll on nature that extended beyond national borders and required "international solutions."
"What has occurred in our region as a result of wars of aggression is ecocide, or environmental genocide, and it must be condemned," Pezeshkian said at a nature summit in Armenia that was attended by United Nations Secretary-General Antonio Guterres.
Pezeshkian decried an "atmosphere of distrust" between Iran and the U.S. in a recent interview with CBS News .
"As long as they don't cease doing what they're imposing on us every day, it's difficult to trust them," he said. "Every day, they increase the sanctions against us. Every day, they threaten us. Every day, they strike us, and they don't honor what they have put in writing. So, if they engage in talks, will they actually follow through?"
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-us-houthi-saudi-arabia-trump/#post-update-ee7946dd
Rubio tells Lebanon's president not to return seized explosives to Hezbollah
Secretary of State Marco Rubio urged Lebanon's president on Thursday not to return several hundred kilograms of explosives which Lebanese authorities seized last week from the Iran-backed militant group Hezbollah, a State Department official told CBS News.
In a call, Rubio asked Lebanese President Joseph Aoun "to hold accountable all those who ordered, financed, and directed the shipment, and underscored that returning explosives to Hezbollah should be out of the question," the official said.
The explosives had been seized on a road linking eastern Lebanon to Beirut, a Hezbollah lawmaker acknowledged earlier Thursday.
Rubio on the call also brought up "Lebanon's 1955 Boycott Law," the official said, which prohibits any contact between Lebanese and Israeli citizens.
Rubio told Aoun the law is "routinely used for politicized prosecutions that deter steps towards peace," the official said. The secretary of state called the law "impractical and urged direct military-to-military communication between Lebanese and Israeli forces for deconfliction," the U.S. official added.
This comes a day after the White House criticized a legal complaint that was filed in Lebanon against the country's ambassador to Washington over her reported outreach to her Israeli counterpart.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-us-houthi-saudi-arabia-trump/#post-update-38eb1eba
White House prepares plans to use weapon dubbed "mother of all bombs" on Iran, sources say
The White House and Pentagon have prepared plans to use a Massive Ordnance Air Blast bomb to hit military sites in Iran if ordered by President Trump, sources familiar with the matter told CBS News.
A Pentagon official told CBS News: "The Department of War plans for a wide range of operational scenarios to execute Presidential and Secretary level direction and to ensure mission success in any operational environment."
A White House spokesperson said: "We do not comment on sensitive military plans that may or may not exist. President Trump keeps all options on the table to ensure that Iran can never have a nuclear weapon."
Last week, Mr. Trump said the U.S. would not attack Iran before the midterm elections. He also touted "productive conversations" with Iran, though he did not provide specifics.
CNN first reported Thursday on the plans to use the Massive Ordnance Air Blast Bomb, or MOAB, on Iran.
Mr. Trump set a precedent for using the MOAB, also known as the "mother of all bombs," when it was used in 2017 to attack an Islamic State cave complex in Afghanistan. The bomb is the largest U.S. non-nuclear weapon, weighing upwards of 30,000 pounds at a cost of $170,000 per unit.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-us-houthi-saudi-arabia-trump/#post-update-dcb18e23
Ousted general warned of years-long missile defense shortage before Iran war
In late December, two months before the U.S. war with Iran began, Army Chief of Staff Gen. Randy George warned that his service was critically short on missile defense munitions and that the shortage would last another five years, according to multiple U.S. officials familiar with the matter. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth ousted George in April, as the Iran war was underway.
George's risk analysis appeared in the Pentagon's Global Force Management Allocation Plan, a defense secretary-approved annual order determining where troops and weapons will be allocated across the globe, according to the U.S. officials who spoke to CBS News.
He warned that the stockpile of Terminal High Altitude Area Defense batteries and Patriot missiles would continue to be strategically low until 2030. His assessment was written for the Defense Department's plan for fiscal year 2027 and before the outbreak of the U.S.-Israel-led war with Iran, now in its seventh month.
U.S. officials who spoke to CBS News under condition of anonymity because they were not authorized to speak publicly said his assessment stemmed from the transfer of Patriots to Ukraine after Russia's invasion in 2022, the expenditure of munitions to defend Israel, and the reduced capacity of the defense industrial base.

The Saudi International, the final event of the Asian Tour golf season, was postponed on Thursday following several deadly attacks on the kingdom by Yemen's Iran-backed Houthis.
The tournament was originally scheduled to take place from Nov. 18 to 21 in Riyadh and no alternative date has been announced.
"The Asian Tour remains in direct contact with its players and members regarding the schedule and next steps," read a joint statement from Saudi Golf and the Asian Tour, saying the competition was postponed due to "logistical challenges."
The tournament, which was part of the European Tour from 2019 to 2021, is backed by Saudi Arabia's Private Investment Fund.
The postponement follows that of the Six Kings Slam tennis exhibition, which was pushed back from next week until December on Wednesday.
Earlier this week, three Saudi Pro League football matches, including one involving Cristiano Ronaldo's Al Nassr, were also postponed.

Lebanese president asks Rubio to press Israel to stop "assaults" in southern Lebanon
Lebanese President Joseph Aoun asked U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio to press Israel on its assaults on southern Lebanon and stand by the trilateral agreement signed in June .
The president's office said on X it received a call from Rubio "during which they discussed the general situation in Lebanon and the region in light of recent developments."
Aoun said he requested Israel stop bulldozing operations, enable farmers to use their fields and stop targeting them, according to the Lebanese president's office.
Israel patrols a so-called "security zone" in southern Lebanon to prevent attacks by the Iran-backed Hezbollah militant group. As part of the trilateral agreement, Israel was supposed to progressively give control over to official Lebanese army forces. However, Israel has said they will only do so when Hezbollah completely disarms, which the group said it will not do.
Aoun told Rubio that Lebanese forces have been "fully carrying out their duties in implementing decisions related to preventing armed displays" in a nod to them carrying out their part of the trilateral agreement.
The State Department has not yet released a readout of the call from its side.

A Saudi foreign ministry official on Thursday accused Iran of encouraging the Houthis in their ongoing fight with the kingdom, adding Tehran continues to arm and train the group.
"Iran continues to arm, train and support the Houthi militia in violation of U.N. security council resolutions," Rayed Krimly, deputy minister for public diplomacy at the foreign ministry, said during a virtual briefing with reporters, according to the AFP news agency.
"We think that the escalation by the Houthis at this latest stage of the conflict is encouraged by Iran," he added later.
Saudi Arabia said Thursday it had intercepted a ballistic missile in the Medina area, home to one of the holiest cities in Islam. The Houthis swiftly denied they were responsible.

Houthi attack on presidential palace in Yemen's Aden kills 4: AFP sources
The Houthis carried out a missile attack on the Yemeni government's presidential palace in the interim capital of Aden on Thursday, with government military sources reporting four killed.
Residents of the southern city reported hearing explosions on Thursday, while a journalist saw smoke rising from the site, which also serves as the headquarters of the internationally recognized government.
Three local police sources said on condition of anonymity that "Houthi missiles targeted the Al-Maashiq presidential palace."
Four people were killed and 10 others wounded in the attack, government military sources later said.
Media affiliated with the pro-Iran Houthis said they had targeted "the headquarters of military operations, led by Saudi officers, at the Al-Maashiq Palace in Aden, as well as weapons depots belonging to them."
Government forces also said on social media Thursday that the Houthis had fired "several ballistic missiles" at the central city of Marib, which sits in a strategic oil-rich region.

At least 10 vessels have been attacked in or near the Strait of Hormuz since Sunday, a high since war broke out between the United States, Israel and Iran, security data showed Wednesday.
Four vessels were struck on Tuesday and six on Sunday &mdash; the highest number of attacks in a single day since the start of the conflict &mdash; according to data from security firm Vanguard, the International Maritime Organization and the British maritime agency UKMTO.
Two seafarers have been reported missing and a container ship reportedly sank as a result of recent attacks, according to the maritime sources.
Merchant vessels transiting the vital passage were first targeted by Iran in March in retaliation for U.S.-Israeli strikes on its territory, and Tehran has continued to strike vessels passing through the strait via routes it has not approved.
The attacks have disrupted shipping through the strait, which is normally one of the busiest routes for exporting oil and other commodities from Gulf countries.
Twenty-three attacks have been recorded since the start of October, almost double the average monthly number of incidents since the start of the war.
"Gulf oil flows have been recovering, with traffic through Hormuz recently returning to around 80 percent of pre-war levels despite the conflict," Vanguard analyst Barun Gupta told AFP on Wednesday.
"Increasing the tempo of attacks therefore gives Tehran a way of demonstrating that it can still disrupt that recovery and impose costs on commercial shipping and global energy markets," Gupta continued.

The escalating war between the Iran-backed Houthis and Saudi-backed Yemeni government forces is cutting off lifesaving food supplies to about 800,000 people in the region, according to the U.N. World Food Programme .
Many parts of Taiz, Yemen's third-largest city, which is located in Houthi-controlled territory, is completely inaccessible to the nonprofit, the WFP said. There are more than 600,000 people, including 200,000 women and children, in Taiz who depend on humanitarian aid, according to the agency.
"We are critically concerned about the escalating conflict, but even more so about the growing number of people we can no longer reach because of it," El-Khidir Daloum, WFP representative and country director in Yemen, said in a statement Wednesday. "Many of the families affected have been displaced multiple times and were already struggling to afford even basic food items. This is the kind of situation that can rapidly spiral into a humanitarian catastrophe if assistance cannot move safely and reach those who need it most."
The hunger situation in Yemen is among the worst in the world. Ten out of 16 districts in Yemen are categorized as being at emergency levels of food insecurity, the WFP said. About 500,000 children in Yemen under the age of 5 suffer from severe acute malnutrition, according to the group.
There are also 37 WFP employees currently being held by the Houthis, the group said, calling for their immediate release.

U.S. inflation accelerated to 3.7% as energy costs soared
Inflation accelerated across the U.S. in September, rising at an annual rate of 3.7% as energy prices soared, according to new government data.
Inflation picked up after two months of holding steady at an annual pace of 3.4%. Core CPI, which excludes the more volatile energy and gas categories, rose 2.6% from a year ago, accelerating from a 2.4% rate in August and higher than economists' forecast of 2.5%.
Inflation has risen sharply since the start of the year due to soaring energy prices, sparked by the war in Iran and Russia's war with Ukraine.
The Labor Department said Wednesday that gasoline prices, which rose 9.1% in September from the prior month, accounted for more than half the monthly increase in the CPI report. Energy prices rose 20.4% from a year ago, while gasoline jumped 34.2%.
Prices at the pump have cooled slightly in recent weeks but remain elevated. The national average for a gallon of gasoline was $4.33 a gallon on Wednesday, AAA reported, up from $2.98 before the war started. The national average for diesel, which hit a record high in late September, was $6.29 a gallon.

Saudis say another Houthi ballistic missile targeting Riyadh was intercepted
A spokesperson for the Saudi-led coalition forces said Wednesday they had intercepted an attack from the Iran-backed Houthi militia.
Coalition spokesman Turki al-Maliki said in a post on X a ballistic missile "launched by the terrorist Houthi militia towards Riyadh has been intercepted and destroyed."
Earlier, residents in the Saudi capital had reported hearing explosions.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-us-houthi-saudi-arabia-trump/#post-update-91b26f5d
Residents in Saudi Arabia's capital say they have heard new explosions in Riyadh
Residents in Saudi Arabia's capital say they have heard new explosions in Riyadh.
The residents spoke on condition of anonymity for their safety. One lives in northern Riyadh, where King Khalid International Airport is located.
The source of the explosions wasn't immediately clear. The Saudi civil defense reported air raid alerts in Riyadh and Kharj province southeast of the capital. Kharj is home to the state-owned Military Industries Corporation and Prince Sultan Air Base.
The airport in Riyadh has been targeted several times in the past week with deadly attacks. Iran-backed Houthi rebels in neighboring Yemen have claimed responsibility.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-us-houthi-saudi-arabia-trump/#post-update-0e453a21
Intercepted missiles over Riyadh injure at least 4 people, officials say
Saudi Arabia's Civil Defense said debris from two intercepted Houthi missiles fell on residential areas in Riyadh on Tuesday, injuring four people and damaging 15 homes.
State-run Saudi Press Agency reported the injured included two women, a man and a child. Images released by Saudi's Civil Defense showed broken windows caused by what it said was fallen shrapnel.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-us-houthi-saudi-arabia-trump/#post-update-cc59e341
Building fire reported in Aramco compound targeted by Houthis, Saudi officials say
The Saudi Ministry of Energy said Tuesday a limited fire broke out in an administrative building in Aramco, one of the world's largest oil facilities, that was targeted in the country's Eastern Province. The ministry said no injuries were reported.
The attack comes as the Iranian-backed Houthis and the Saudi coalition exchanged attacks Tuesday night. It marks a fresh escalation in the widening Middle East crisis and came shortly after the Saudi coalition reported a series of alerts for incoming missiles heading for the country's Eastern Province, a major oil-producing region and the capital Riyadh.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-us-houthi-saudi-arabia-trump/#post-update-d8c90ab9
Saudi-led forces confirm strikes on Yemen oil sites
The Saudi-led coalition battling the Iran-backed Houthis confirmed it struck oil sites across western Yemen on Tuesday, including in the capital Sanaa, in response to the deadliest attack on the kingdom's soil in years.
Blasts were heard shortly after in Saudi capital Riyadh and at an oil giant Aramco facility in eastern Saudi Arabia, witnesses and journalists told AFP.
Tuesday's coalition strikes in Yemen's Sanaa and the western provinces of Dhamar and Saada targeted facilities used to "supply the war effort" by the Houthis to "threaten the kingdom, Yemen, the southern Red Sea and waterways," coalition spokesman Turki al-Maliki wrote on X.
An AFP correspondent in Sanaa reported an explosion, while a breaking news alert on the Houthi-affiliated Al-Masirah TV channel said earlier that "Saudi aggression" targeted the oil firm.
https://www.cbsnews.com/live-updates/iran-war-us-houthi-saudi-arabia-trump/#post-update-1e8f4cb6

Donald Trump waives sanctions, tariffs on purchase of Russian oil and gas - The Hindu
Account subscription benefits alongside Premium Stories, Editorials,
Donald Trump waives sanctions, tariffs on purchase of Russian oil and gas
Trump said he had submitted to Congress a certification that issuing such a waiver at this time is in the national interest of the United States
Updated - October 17, 2026 01:17 pm IST - Washington
US President Donald Trump gestures as he attends a MAGA Inc. dinner at Mar-a-Lago resort, in Palm Beach, on October 16, 2026
U.S. President Donald Trump has issued a waiver from sanctions, restrictions and duties under the Sanctioning Russia and Iran Act , citing national interest.
In an executive order on Friday night, Trump said that it is in the US “national interests” to withhold some of the sanctions.
“I am hereby waiving the application of such sanctions, restrictions, and duties provided for in the Graham Act,” Mr. Trump said.
Mr. Trump said he had submitted to Congress a certification that issuing such a waiver at this time is in the national interest of the United States.
The Lindsey O Graham Sanctioning Russia and Iran Act empowered Mr. Trump to impose sweeping sanctions on Moscow and slap steep tariffs against the world’s largest purchasers of Russian oil and gas, including China and India.
The Graham Act, as it is now called, was passed by the U.S. Congress on September 16 and signed into law by Mr. Trump two days later.
Trump says Russia to immediately supply diesel to U.S. after talks with Putin
The law authorised Mr. Trump to impose tariffs of up to 100% within 30 days of its enactment on all goods brought into the U.S. from the five largest importers of Russian crude oil or gas.
The law also has a provision in Section 115 that allows the President to grant waiver from the application of sanctions, restrictions and duties if such a step is in the national interest of the U.S.
“Pursuant to section 115(a) of the Graham Act, I hereby waive the application of any duties provided for in sections 112 and 113 of the Graham Act or this order,” Mr. Trump said.
Section 113 of the Graham Act deals with imposition of tariffs upto 100% on countries that purchase Russian origin crude oil or natural gas or facilitate sanctions evasion.
The waiver from application of the act comes days after President Trump struck a deal with Russia for supply of diesel to the U.S. and global markets.
It also comes days ahead of U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio’s visit to India.
Double standards: On the Trump diesel deal with Russia
External Affairs Minister S. Jaishankar had raised concerns about the Graham Act during a meeting with Rubio on the sidelines of the UN General Assembly in New York last month.
Mr. Jaishankar is understood to have conveyed to Mr. Rubio that oil refineries in India were configured largely for handling medium sour crude, which is supplied by West Asia countries and Russia.
He also told Mr. Rubio that Venezuela supplies heavier grade crude and the US has light grade crude which would require blending before being used by refineries in India.
With oil supplies from West Asia countries hit due to the Iran war, India has to depend on Russian crude to keep its economy running.

Ukraine’s Volodymyr Zelenskyy has asked the leaders for another €70bn next year to fund Ukraine’s defence against Russia as he joined a key EU summit in Brussels ( 10:10 , 13:44 , 15:13 , 15:26 , 16:00 ).
EU’s Ursula von der Leyen and António Costa confirmed the bloc will look at the request ( 19:01 , 19:14 , 19:20 ), as they said the EU’s support to Ukraine “matters more than ever” given growing pressures from Russia.
During a press conference in Brussels, Zelenskyy responded to US president Donald Trump’s criticism of his actions ( 13:51 , 13:56 , 14:04 ), and confirmed that Starlink satellite systems were back up, but with speeds not completely restored to previous levels ( 13:42 ).
Starlink access still not fully restored for Ukraine’s military, says Zelenskyy
Zelenskyy smiled but offered no response to a suggestion he was being used as a “scapegoat” for Donald Trump’s domestic problems ahead of next month’s midterm elections ( 14:06 ).
Zelenskyy smiles when asked if he feels he is being made a scapegoat for pressures facing Trump
German chancellor Friedrich Merz said there was a “broad consensus” on the EU’s need to keep supporting Ukraine throughout this coming winter, acknowledging Kyiv’s request for further financial assistance ( 16:45 ), and appearing to back retaliatory strikes against Russian communication and energy supply systems ( 16:49 , 16:54 ).
Several other EU leaders offered their backing for Ukraine, ( 10:10 , 10:21 , 10:25 , 10:36 , 11:09 , 11:23 ), with top EU figures saying the bloc would not lift any sanctions on Russia unless it stops the war ( 10:10 ), and criticising Trump’s diesel deal with Russia ( 10:10 , 19:32 ).
Zelenskyy declined to say if Ukraine will be targeting Russian refineries ( 13:47 ).
Power plant attacks reflect Russian blueprint to make Ukrainian cities unliveable
EU’s Costa insisted the new EU budget needed to be agreed by the end of the year, despite calls to delay its adoption considering a number of big elections in the EU ( 19:27 ).
The European Commission president, Ursula von der Leyen, insisted the bloc needed a bigger budget with “flexibilities” to respond to various crises ( 19:12 ).
Germany’s Merz conceded there were still “significant differences” between EU capitals on the budget, with talks expected to continue until the end of the year ( 16:43 , 16:54 , 16:59 ).
Pope Leo spoke with the Russian president, Vladimir Putin, this afternoon, expressing his grave concern for the humanitarian situation in the Russian-occupied Ukrainian city of Oleshky ( 17:36 ).
The Kremlin said Putin expressed his support for Leo’s ideas, but blamed Ukraine for the situation in the city ( 18:22 ).

Speaking at the same time, France’s Emmanuel Macron told reporters that he did not consider a diesel deal struck by Trump with Russia as useful.
“ This decision is not good for ‌anyone. It sends a ​negative signal. It a an element ⁠of division and ​doubt,” Macron ​said after ​an EU ​summit ‌in ​Brussels, Reuters reported.
After some concluding comments on the bloc’s response to the growing sales of Chinese electric cars in Europe , von der Leyen and Costa wrap up their press conference.
Elsewhere, US president Donald Trump insisted that neither him nor Elon Musk was responsible for Ukraine losing Starlink access.
Starlink access still not fully restored for Ukraine’s military, says Zelenskyy
He claimed the restrictions was not imposed by the US, and that he has a good relationship with Ukraine’s Zelenskyy, Reuters reported.
Costa gets asked if he is more confident that a deal can be found by the end of the year.
And there is also a question about Marine Le Pen’s suggestions that the budget should not be decided this early given so many key elections in Europe next year that are likely to reshape the political landscape of the continent.
Costa repeats Merz’s point from earlier that it usually takes about a year to implement a budget ( 16:59 ), and anything that would threaten to delay its entry into force from January 2028 puts it a risk altogether.
“This has nothing [to do] with elections,” he says, and adds if that was a consideration, there would never be a good moment as there are always elections somewhere.
“But unfortunately, the life is not only elections. We are talking about our farmers, our companies, our students, our researchers, our universities – and they are waiting for new European funds.”
Asked about Ukraine’s new financial needs, von der Leyen says that 2026 is covered, but confirms the gap for 2027 is around €70bn euros.
On migration, von der Leyen says all leaders recognise “enormous progress” made on this issue in recent years, but more needs to be done.
He says the number of illegal crossings has gone down by over 56% in the last two years, and down by 34% in the first nine months of this year.
“These trends need to continue to remain successful.”
She says there is a “new phenomenon” of state and non-state actors trying to “engineer sudden mass movements of people … to overwhelm the authorities.”
It will come as news to the likes of Poland and Lithuania that it’s anything new given the pressure from Belarus for years.
She says the EU’s support “matters more than ever.”
She says the bloc “is doing a long, but the reality is simple: as long as this war continues, Ukraine’s financing needs will keep growing.”
“Right now, the European Union and its member states account for 80% of external aid to Ukraine, and we will continue to lead. But we urgently need also other partners to step in.”
“As throughout these five years of war, no one wants more peace than the people of Ukraine, and it is our duty to support them , to be in the position to find a just and sustainable peace for Ukraine.”
Von der Leyen also mounts a defence of the commission’s proposal on the next EU budget, effectively saying that as leaders keep coming to the EU asking it to fix things, it’s understandable that it’ll need more resources.
“If there’s one lesson we have painfully learned over the last seven years, that is the lesson that we are in a very fast changing and volatile world and that there will be more crises. We just don’t know what is going to be happening. … I could only act in these crisis because of the flexibilities in the budget … and in each crisis, everybody was calling for Europe to step in.”
He says the new proposals have “even less flexibilities,” and so the commission is not happy with them.
“The question before us is not which priorities we should abandon, but how to finance these priorities.”
She points out to high energy price increases since the start of Trump’s war against Iran.
“This is the reality we have to deal with today, and we discussed this intensively with the member states. Because of the fact that the member states have very different energy mixes, we know there is no silver bullet as a solution.”
She says the G7-agreed release of oil and diesel, the EU’s extension of temporary state aid framework, and some other technical changes should provide some relief.
“For the mid term, it is important that as long as Europe depends on imported oil and gas and our prosperity remains exposed to the events beyond our control, it is important for us to stay the course of the transition; that is a transition towards affordable, clean and homegrown energy sources, … nuclear and renewables.”
She talks about the need to “modernise and improve our grid network” in Europe .
She also briefly discusses proposed changes to the ETS system and the EU’s trade with China, saying there was a “remarkable unity” on the latter point to reduce the “unsustainable” bilateral trade deficit.
Finally, on migration, Costa says the system is “working but … must still be improved.”
“Migration remains a very political issue where there are different political visions which are known to all of you. The European Council will continue to work on this issue,” he says.
On the next EU budget, Costa says the negotiations will need to conclude by the end of the year, and there was some progress made.
But with lots of diplospeak, he admits that there are still some differences between different countries, and so he needs to “accelerate” the work on this issue.
On Ukraine , he says “Ukraine remains a central priority for Europe.”
“ Our resolve to stand by Ukraine and its people is as strong as ever. At the same time, every genuine step towards peace matters and we support all genuine efforts towards it. Unfortunately, Russia’s action point the other way.”
He calls for “more pressure on the aggression, not less,” and for more financial support for Ukraine, with a request to the commission to propose new funding.
“Supporting Ukraine has never been more relevant as Moscow is intensifying its hybrid campaigns across Europe. We see acts of sabotage and repeated violations of member states airspace in territorial waters.”
He talks about good discussions on cutting red tape and competitiveness of the bloc, and confirms what Merz said earlier about a special meeting on this issue in February next year ( 16:43 ).
On energy, he says that “current volatility on energy prices [is] putting intense pressure on households and businesses.”
“This crisis is triggered by events far beyond our borders, but we are also paying the price of our dependency on fossil fuels and the lack of a single market for energy. Given the urgency of the situation, there cannot be business as usual.”
He says there will be a united EU response to this issue.
He also talks about discussions on trade policy, with “total unity among the member states” on supporting the bloc’s efforts to diversify trade routes. Again, he references Merz’s proposals for a new trade instrument.
“Still someone here,” von der Leyen jokes as she gets to the podium.

### 16. ★★★☆☆ US Stocks Set for Muted Open as Investors Assess Inflation Data and Technology Sector Volatility: Dow Jones, S&P, Nasdaq, Wall Street - Yahoo Finance

- **分类**: 📈 市场
- **来源**: GN Markets / CNBC Top
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T13:13:23+00:00
- **发生了什么**: US Stocks Set for Muted Open as Investors Assess Inflation Data and Technology Sector Volatility: Dow Jones, S&amp;P, Nasdaq, Wall Street&nbsp;&nbsp;Yahoo Finance

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

US Stocks Set for Muted Open as Investors Assess Inflation Data and Technology Sector Volatility: Dow Jones, S&P, Nasdaq, Wall Street
Dow Jones stock ticker ©Adobe Stock Images US stock futures pointed to a broadly unchanged opening on Friday as investors assessed recent market volatility, stronger-than-expected inflation figures and the outlook for technology shares.
Wall Street recovered sharply during Thursday's session after initially extending Wednesday's technology-led sell-off. The Nasdaq rebounded from its lowest intraday level in almost a month, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average reached its highest closing level in a month.
Despite the recovery, futures suggested a cautious start to Friday's trading, with investors continuing to monitor Treasury yields and expectations for Federal Reserve interest rate policy.
US equities experienced substantial fluctuations during the week, beginning with relatively subdued trading before technology shares came under pressure on Wednesday.
The selling continued into Thursday's opening session, pushing the Nasdaq to its lowest intraday level in nearly a month.
However, buying interest emerged as the session progressed, allowing all three major US indices to finish higher.
The Dow Jones Industrial Average climbed 556.21 points, or 1.1%, to close at 52,177.69, supported by gains in Nike (NYSE:NKE), Salesforce (NYSE:CRM) and Home Depot (NYSE:HD).
The S&P 500 advanced 25.12 points, or 0.3%, to 7,744.06.
The technology-heavy Nasdaq Composite recovered from earlier losses to finish 20.16 points, or 0.1%, higher at 26,713.03.
The rebound followed a period of selling pressure linked to renewed concerns about valuations and investment expectations in the artificial intelligence sector.
Federal Reserve Chair Highlights Economic Significance of AI
Technology stocks may receive some support from comments by Federal Reserve Chair Kevin Warsh during the annual meetings of the International Monetary Fund and World Bank in Bangkok.
Warsh described artificial intelligence as "the most profound economic transformation of a lifetime", highlighting its potential long-term implications for the US and global economies.
His remarks addressed the broader economic importance of AI at a time when investors have been reassessing the outlook for companies associated with the technology.
However, Warsh did not provide specific guidance on upcoming Federal Reserve interest rate decisions.
Investors therefore remain focused on economic data and market expectations for the central bank's next policy announcement.
Recent inflation figures have complicated the outlook for monetary policy.
The US Labor Department reported that its producer price index for final demand increased by 1.3% in September, following an upwardly revised 0.5% rise in August.
Economists had expected a monthly increase of 0.5%.
On an annual basis, producer price inflation accelerated to 6.3% from an upwardly revised 5.6% in August, exceeding market expectations.
Additional data released on Friday showed that US import and export prices also increased substantially more than economists had forecast in September.
Despite the stronger inflation readings, US stock futures remained relatively unchanged following the latest report.
According to CME Group's FedWatch Tool, market expectations continued to indicate that the Federal Reserve would leave interest rates unchanged at its forthcoming policy meeting later in October.
Thursday's recovery in US equities coincided with a sharp decline in Treasury yields.
The yield on the benchmark 10-year US Treasury note fell to its lowest level in three weeks, despite the stronger-than-expected producer price inflation figures.
Lower bond yields can support equity valuations by reducing borrowing costs and the discount rates applied to future corporate earnings.
The decline may have contributed to renewed buying interest following the earlier weakness in technology shares.
However, the persistence of inflationary pressures remains an important consideration for investors assessing the future direction of interest rates.
Gold, Airlines and Housing Stocks Lead Sector Recovery
Several sectors recorded substantial gains during Thursday's session.
Gold mining shares advanced alongside the price of the precious metal, lifting the NYSE Arca Gold Bugs Index by 4.7%.
The increase followed the index's decline to its lowest closing level in more than two months during the previous session.
Airline stocks also recovered, with the NYSE Arca Airline Index rising 3.8% after reaching a six-month closing low on Wednesday.
Housing-related equities posted strong gains, pushing the Philadelphia Housing Sector Index up 3.6%.
Biotechnology, healthcare and pharmaceutical stocks also attracted buying interest.
However, the recovery was not uniform across the market.
Computer hardware shares remained under pressure, with the NYSE Arca Computer Hardware Index declining 2.5% to its lowest closing level in well over a month.
Investors Await Direction Following Volatile Sessions
Friday's relatively flat futures suggested that investors were taking a more cautious approach after the sharp market movements of the previous two sessions.
Thursday's recovery demonstrated renewed buying interest across several sectors, although technology stocks continued to face uncertainty following the recent sell-off.
The stronger inflation data also raised questions about the outlook for US monetary policy, even as market expectations continued to favour unchanged interest rates at the Federal Reserve's next meeting.
Investors will be monitoring Treasury yields, technology sector performance and further economic developments for indications of the market's near-term direction.

</details>

### 17. ★★★☆☆ Trump denies limiting Ukraine access to Starlink network

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World / GN Geopolitics / NPR World
- **多源确认**: 3
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T19:33:48+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Speed curbs limited Ukraine's defences against deadly Russian attacks on Tuesday night.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

US President Donald Trump has denied limiting Ukraine&#x27;s access to Starlink&#x27;s sister internet network Starshield after it restricted drone speed, hampering Kyiv&#x27;s ability to protect itself from Russian attacks.
"It wasn&#x27;t me and I don&#x27;t think it was [Starlink owner] Elon [Musk]," Trump said, hours after Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky said the usual speed had yet to be restored.
Musk himself suggested the US government might have been behind the restrictions, while news agency Reuters reported on Thursday that the order had come from inside the White House.
Ukraine was hit by several deadly strikes on Tuesday after the network slowdown.
Starlink is a civilian-use, high-speed internet network operated by Musk&#x27;s SpaceX. Starshield - which relies on Starlink - is its military-grade counterpart that was designed for the Pentagon.
The US had previously warned it could withhold sharing intelligence, crucial for Kyiv&#x27;s defences, if Ukraine did not stop hitting Russian oil refineries.
Tension has been brewing between Washington and Kyiv for days over a recent controversial diesel deal between Russia and the US.
Zelensky - angered by the deal - resumed targeting Russian oil refineries this week in a move criticised by Trump three days after he announced a ceasefire on energy infrastructure. Kyiv said it would stop the strikes if Russia did the same but Moscow never confirmed the ceasefire and continued its attacks.
"If I don&#x27;t stand up to this... who else will?" Zelensky asked EU leaders at a summit on Friday.
Trump had also said that Zelensky was "more difficult to deal with than [Russian President Vladimir] Putin".
But speaking to reporters as he left the White House on Friday, the US president said he had a good relationship with his Ukrainian counterpart.
Asked about Starshield, he said he was "not sure" it "ever came down".
Musk earlier explained that Starshield was managed by the US government and that the Pentagon could have been responsible.
In its report about the alleged order regarding Starshield, Reuters quoted two unnamed US officials and a Western official briefed on the matter.
The US government has allowed Ukraine to use Starlink and Starshield in its defence against Russia. Musk cut Moscow&#x27;s access to the service in February, giving Kyiv an advantage in the conflict.
On Tuesday night, Starlink restricted speed limits to 100mph (160km/h), meaning any weapons that travelled faster than that lost connectivity to the network.
Some 600,000 in Ukraine were left without power as communications and energy facilities used by Ukraine&#x27;s military were also hit in the deadly attacks.
A bipartisan group of US senators has signed a letter demanding an investigation into the disruptions to the network.
"Restrictions reportedly degraded Ukraine&#x27;s ability to conduct defensive operations against Russian drone and missile attacks on civilian infrastructure," the letter read.
On Friday, French President Emmanuel Macron vowed that EU states would not abandon Ukraine.
Macron told a two-day summit in Brussels attended by Zelensky that it was Europe&#x27;s job to hold the line in the face of some people&#x27;s "ambiguities". That was apparently a reference to signs of wavering US support for Ukraine.
Funding for Ukraine and helping it get through another freezing winter - its fifth since Russia&#x27;s full-scale invasion in 2022 - topped the summit&#x27;s agenda.
The war will cost â¬155bn (Â£131bn; $174bn) next year, Zelensky told the European Council, with at least â¬70bn needed to come from the EU for defence and weapons.
Macron said Europe was working to help Ukraine with its satellite and early warning systems, and Ukraine&#x27;s friends would look at its request for further aid.

### 18. ★★★☆☆ The European Union moves to toughen its trade policy toward China, amid Chinese warnings of a "trade war." - صوت الإمارات

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: GN China
- **多源确认**: 1

- **发生了什么**: A European strategy to protect industry, and a daily deficit in the billions
Brussels, Belgium &#x2013; The European Union is moving steadily toward imposing a new round of restrictions and firm measures on imports from China. This effort aims to address a widening trade deficit and protect domestic industrial sectors from competition that Brussels deems &#x201C;unfair.&#x201D; The move is bolster

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

A European strategy to protect industry, and a daily deficit in the billions
Brussels, Belgium &#x2013; The European Union is moving steadily toward imposing a new round of restrictions and firm measures on imports from China. This effort aims to address a widening trade deficit and protect domestic industrial sectors from competition that Brussels deems &#x201C;unfair.&#x201D; The move is bolstered by an unprecedented level of consensus and influence between France and Germany.
A European strategy to protect industry, and a daily deficit in the billions
Following the European Union summit in Brussels, European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen stated that the trade deficit in goods with China exceeds one billion euros per day (more than 360 billion euros annually). She emphasized that the Commission is considering the development of additional protective measures, covering a wide range of sectors such as automobiles, chemicals, plastics, iron alloys, robotics, solar energy, and wind energy. Furthermore, these measures also encompass medical technologies.
The unified European stance has taken shape through a convergence between Paris and Berlin, with French President Emmanuel Macron and German leader Friedrich Merz calling for the establishment of robust deterrent measures to counter Chinese state subsidies and excess production capacity. Furthermore, the proposal includes the introduction of a new mechanism&#x2014;modeled after &#x201C;Section 301&#x201D; of the U.S. Trade Act&#x2014;that would grant the bloc broad authority to impose import quotas or deny trading partners access to its single market, which comprises 450 million consumers.
Although the two sides have reached a draft agreement to cap imports of Chinese hybrid vehicles at 350,000 units per year&#x2014;a move that would reduce Chinese exports by more than half&#x2014;European Trade Commissioner Maro&#x161; &#x160;ef&#x10D;ovi&#x10D; emphasized that this agreement is merely a starting point for restoring financial balance. A new round of talks has also been scheduled for next January.
In contrast, China&#x2019;s Ministry of Commerce warned the European bloc against underestimating its capacity to defend its interests, rejecting claims that European pressure had forced it to make concessions. Beijing also urged Brussels to avoid sliding into an open trade war, pointing to its significant leverage over the supply of rare earth minerals&#x2014;a category from which the European Union sources approximately 46% of its total requirements. These minerals are critical to sensitive European industries, including the production of permanent magnets, electric vehicles, and defense equipment.

Image caption, Defence Minister Guido Crosetto has warned against showing any weakness to Russia&#x27;s Vladimir Putin
By Sarah Rainsford Eastern and Southern Europe correspondent ,Â  Reporting from Rome
Italy&#x27;s defence minister has said it was a "complete mistake, a major mistake" for the US to limit access to satellite systems and intelligence used by Ukraine to defend itself against Russian attacks.
Guido Crosetto warned in a BBC interview that any such move by US President Donald Trump&#x27;s administration "makes absolutely no sense" and risked prolonging Russia&#x27;s full-scale invasion rather than helping to bring peace.
US officials have said Trump has not asked for the slowdown of internet speeds the US provides to Ukraine and which are vital for its war effort. Trump has also denied that the US has stopped sharing early-warning alerts of incoming Russian aerial attacks with Ukraine.
Crosetto&#x27;s comments continue Italy&#x27;s sharpening criticism of the US.
Earlier this week, Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni called Trump&#x27;s deal to buy diesel from Russia a "very risky choice" and said it would "grant Russia legitimacy" as it bombed cities in Ukraine.
Trump agreed the deal with Russian President Vladimir Putin, blaming Kyiv&#x27;s own attacks on Russian refineries for rising fuel prices.
Meloni, once considered so close to the US president she was dubbed a "Trump whisperer", said the move ran counter to efforts to bring Putin to the negotiating table.
"Peace will come when Putin is convinced there is a stalemate, that he cannot advance any further," her defence minister told the BBC at his ministry building in Rome.
Crosetto was a co-founder of Meloni&#x27;s hard-right Fratelli d&#x27;Italia party, which has led a right-wing government coalition since 2022, shortly after the start of Russia&#x27;s full-scale war.
"If [Putin] sees weakness on the Ukrainian side â and if the US deprives Ukraine of resources, that creates weakness â then he will believe he can win. So he will continue the war," Crosetto warned.
Image caption, Italy&#x27;s prime minister has become a close ally of Ukraine&#x27;s Volodymyr Zelensky
Despite widespread public scepticism, Meloni&#x27;s Italian government has been a strong ally of Ukraine from the start, sending both military and humanitarian aid.
The specifics of those deliveries have always been classified.
Crosetto vowed that Italian military support would continue "for as long as this government is in place", though he warned that getting Italy&#x27;s parliament to approve further deliveries would be "more difficult" as elections approached.
The vote has to take place by the end of 2027 and opinion polls currently put a new far-right party, Futuro Nazionale, ahead of all Meloni&#x27;s coalition partners.
That party&#x27;s leader, former general Roberto Vannacci, is openly sympathetic to Russia and opposes aiding Ukraine, arguing that Europe is wasting its money as Russia&#x27;s military "continues to advance" on the ground.
At a recent event in Tuscany, he described himself as "neither pro-Putin nor pro-Russian" but "pro-Italian".
Crosetto brushed off questions about Vannacci, including on whether his views reflected those of many Italians, saying he had more "serious matters to deal with".
But, perhaps to underline the difference, he was unequivocal on Russia.
"When one state crosses another state&#x27;s border with tanks, when it bombs hospitals and schools, kills civilians and bombs power plants, and has been doing so for four years, I can only describe that as evil," he said.
Zelensky says Ukraine has right to defend itself after Trump calls latest strikes a &#x27;disgrace&#x27;
Inside Ukrainian offensive pushing Russia back - with Starlink playing a key role
But Italians, like many Europeans, are seeing prices go up, driven by the high cost of energy.
During street protests in Rome on Friday, where firecrackers were thrown, secondary school students complained that resources were being diverted to arms instead of education.
Some chanted: "We want more classroom, not more bombs."
In southern Italy, truckers have begun blocking roads complaining at the hike in fuel prices.
Polls here regularly show that a majority of Italians want an end to military aid for Ukraine. One, conducted for the IxÃ¨ institute in September, put support for continuing to supply arms at just 18%.
There are many factors behind that, from a long-standing pacifist tradition to a history of strong business ties to Moscow. Unlike Poland or the Baltic states, Italy feels physically distant from the danger, too.
But the defence minister also blamed an active campaign of disinformation by Moscow â targeting, he said, "certain political figures... and narratives to weaken the political climate against Russia".
"In some way they shift public opinion," the minister argued.
"It&#x27;s very hard to explain to a citizen who&#x27;s having trouble at the petrol pump that we can&#x27;t give any concessions to Russia right now. But that&#x27;s the only way to defend the future of that very citizen who&#x27;s complaining."
Italy has not been targeted for major acts of sabotage, unlike other allies of Ukraine in Europe.
The minister said a fire at an arms depot outside Rome in August was not arson, but "human stupidity".
A report last month that Russia was planning to launch drone attacks at Italy, France and Spain from the container ships in the Mediterranean was not proven, Crosetto also told us.
But cyber-attacks, which he linked to Russia, were now routine - "there are hundreds every day" - and he believed Europe had been too weak in its response.
"You can&#x27;t win a war... if you don&#x27;t fight back," the minister said.

Ship-to-Ship Oil Transfers Spread to India's West Coast as Hormuz Congestion Builds
Ship-to-ship transfers of crude oil are spreading beyond the waters outside the Strait of Hormuz to India's West Coast as congestion builds at established transfer zones, according to Vortexa data cited by The National. The Gulf of Kutch handled 82 cargoes, or roughly 680,000 barrels per day, transferred onto larger vessels in September, the vessel-tracking data showed.
The Gulf of Kutch opens toward the Arabian Sea facing the Gulf of Oman and has become an additional transfer point for Gulf oil producers seeking to keep supplies moving to Asian markets despite heightened risks to tanker journeys through the Strait of Hormuz. Vortexa senior market analyst Varaleka Pant told The National that the nearest transfer zones off Fujairah in the UAE and Sohar in Oman are reaching operational limits, pushing activity as far as India's West Coast.
The congestion just outside Hormuz indicates that the shuttle-shipping arrangement Gulf producers have used to keep exporting through the strait has operational constraints. Cargoes may face delays, or transfers may need to occur farther from the strait.
Analysts differ on how extensively transfers are shifting eastward toward the Indian Ocean, but they agree that congestion at the Oman and UAE zones is leading some producers and buyers to seek transfers farther along the route to Asia. Pant indicated that the transfer operation itself carries relatively low risk, with the greater risk lying in moving barrels out of the western side of Hormuz to the transfer zone.
Recent pricing for Middle Eastern grades reflects the risks tied to moving barrels out of Hormuz. Iraq is offering Basrah Medium for October loading at a discount of $34.50 a barrel and Basrah Heavy at a $37 discount to regional benchmarks, as Baghdad lacks a bypass route around the strait. The UAE, by contrast, commands a premium for its Murban crude, which moves through a pipeline to Fujairah outside the Strait of Hormuz.

### 21. ★★★☆☆ Why did CoreWeave stock get crushed this week? Look to this corner of the debt market for clues

- **分类**: 📈 市场
- **来源**: CNBC Top
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T20:33:55+00:00
- **发生了什么**: CoreWeave’s bond yields and credit default swaps have surged as its stock sells off. Here’s what those credit signals mean for stock investors.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Why did CoreWeave get crushed this week? Look to this corner of the debt market
Mounting fears that CoreWeave&#x27;s debt stress may signal a broader problem with the insatiable borrowing-driven artificial intelligence infrastructure build-out slammed tech stocks this week. The Nasdaq logged a weekly loss of 4.5%. CoreWeave stock plunged nearly 12% on Friday. Shares of the neocloud declined every day except Thursday, losing a quarter of their value since last Friday&#x27;s close. Perhaps even more concerning than the stock rout has been the jump in CoreWeave corporate bond yields and spike in credit default swaps, a kind of insurance policy on debt. Corporate credit quality is getting a lot of attention these days, as funding costs increase due to above-target inflation that just won&#x27;t quit and investor worries about the level of financial commitment being made by companies that don&#x27;t want to be left behind in the AI race. We&#x27;re obviously stock-focused investors around here, but in this wobbly moment for the AI trade, we cannot ignore what&#x27;s happening in the corporate bond market. Credit default swaps help us get a more complete understanding. If they sound familiar, it may be because you&#x27;ve seen the movie "The Big Short," which focuses on the 2008 financial crisis. Hedge-fund manager Michael Burry, portrayed by Christian Bale, used credit default swaps as part of his bet against the U.S. housing industry. This week, the yield on a CoreWeave 2031 bond almost reached 16%. Friday, the yield stood around 14.5%. The instrument was issued in April at a 9.75% yield. At the same time, five-year CoreWeave CDS exceeded 1,000 basis points, up nearly 19% this week and 178% higher than they were 12 months ago. The CDS trading action puts the implied odds of a CoreWeave default within the next five years at around 60%, according to Bloomberg. The holder of debt can buy credit default swaps to protect against any losses resulting from issuer default. Like insurance, the amount paid is tied to the value and quality of the insured asset. To be sure, yields on a debt instrument are tied to the risk of default — when the risk goes up, so does the interest rate. Other factors also matter when it comes to the yield investors demand, such as inflation expectations, liquidity premiums, and the risk-free rate — usually measured by the 10-year Treasury yield , currently nearly 5.1%. A credit default swap, however, only reflects credit risk and none of those other aforementioned sentiment factors. As a result, a CDS can provide a more transparent picture of how investors actually feel about the debt issuer itself. For example, if an investor owns $1 million in debt issued by XYZ Corporation and wants to insure it, they could purchase a CDS. If the quote is 100 basis points, they would pay a premium of $10,000 (on an annual basis) to protect against default. An investor does not need to own the debt to buy a CDS — credit default swaps can also be bought and sold as ways to speculate on improvement or deterioration of a given company&#x27;s credit quality. Apply that to CoreWeave CDS over at 1,000 basis points, and the cost to insure $1 million of CoreWeave debt goes above $100,000. So, why, as stock investors, do we care about insurance rates tied to the bond market? If we think of the things in our everyday lives that require insurance, it starts to make more sense. When you look to insure a home, or car, or anything for that matter, the premium you pay is based on two key factors: (1) the value of the item to be insured, which dictates the max payout in the event of a total loss; and (2) how risky it is to insure, factoring in the odds of the policy ever being triggered and the expected payout (as any residual value can offset the amount the insurer is ultimately on the hook for). It&#x27;s that second point that we as stock investors care about. If the perceived risk of default increases, then that pushes up the quoted price for the CDS. The quoted price therefore acts as an easy-to-watch, real-time, quantifiable proxy for that risk. Obviously, the risk of default is something that should concern all stakeholders because equity value will generally be demolished long before any default actually happens. Those worries about debt risk are what&#x27;s driving the CoreWeave situation. The stock is getting obliterated even though an actual default might never happen. And, according to Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang on Thursday, CoreWeave&#x27;s financial position is stable and "improving every day." Huang&#x27;s comments, which were made to Bloomberg at an event in Stockholm, were credited with reversing Thursday&#x27;s earlier losses in CoreWeave and leading the stock to a higher close. Club name Nvidia is a big CoreWeave shareholder, and CoreWeave uses Nvidia chips to provide its clients with AI compute. Huang said Nvidia does not need to backstop CoreWeave because "their cost of capital is actually decreasing." Remember, risk and reward are tied at the hip. CDS rates, or more importantly, the move in the rate, can provide stock investors with a quick way to see what bond investors think. It&#x27;s not an official credit quality rating, like those issued by S & P Global, Moody&#x27;s, or Fitch. But what they lack in prominence, they make up for in pricing frequency, which can be far more useful to stock investors like us trying to better understand the market overall, as well as the price action of individual securities. Going back to the housing example, say you&#x27;re considering a home on the beach but are concerned about the risk that a natural disaster can come in and damage or destroy the home. You could look to the listing price (think of this like the stock price) and try to figure out risk based on the pricing dynamics (is the house selling for more or less than it last sold, has it been sitting on the market a long time, have the sellers lowered the price to try and increase demand), or you can look into what has happened to the insurance rate on the property. The seller wants to get as much as they can. The insurer, on the other hand, is concerned with the risks posed to the property going forward, something the seller does not care about once it&#x27;s out of their name. Bottom line So, as the AI trade plays out, be sure to watch trading in credit default swaps. It can get complicated, but from the perspective of stock investors, don&#x27;t let that scare you and don&#x27;t overthink it too much. Just remember that, just like insurance premiums, the movement in the CDS market can indicate whether perceived default risk is rising or falling, whether it is increasing or decreasing. While we don&#x27;t think the rates on our names are anywhere near concerning at the moment, even as they are higher due to funding initiatives, where they go from here likely will determine the price action on the equity side. (See here for a full list of the stocks in Jim Cramer&#x27;s Charitable Trust.) As a subscriber to the CNBC Investing Club with Jim Cramer, you will receive a trade alert before Jim makes a trade. Jim waits 45 minutes after sending a trade alert before buying or selling a stock in his charitable trust&#x27;s portfolio. If Jim has talked about a stock on CNBC TV, he waits 72 hours after issuing the trade alert before executing the trade. <details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Kyiv and Kharkiv have experienced rolling blackouts and water outages this week. Photograph: Gleb Garanich/Reuters
Kyiv and Kharkiv have experienced rolling blackouts and water outages this week. Photograph: Gleb Garanich/Reuters
Power plant attacks reflect Russian blueprint to make Ukrainian cities unlivable
Kremlin turning to punishment of Ukraine’s population in major cities as frontlines remain frozen
As Russian missiles and drones have systematically slammed into power plants in Kyiv and Kharkiv this week, the rolling blackouts and water outages have increasingly resembled a supposed Russian blueprint to destroy life in major Ukrainian cities seen by the Guardian.
Even as Donald Trump unilaterally declared an “energy ceasefire” last week between Moscow and Kyiv, Russia continued with its methodical and devastating strikes on Ukraine’s power grid, in particular in Kyiv, in a strategy meant to punish Ukraine’s population and drive the country to capitulation.
Russia and Ukraine trade strikes as Trump ‘energy ceasefire’ collapses
The potential goals of the Russian campaign, according to an assessment seen by the Guardian that Ukrainian intelligence said it intercepted from Russia last month, range from “degrading the quality of life” of Ukrainians through power outages to fully shutting off the water supply, which the document cooly predicts would cause the “deterioration of sanitary conditions and the forced outflow of residents from cities.”
The document does not specify which Russian agency developed the assessment, which is titled Analysis of Ukraine’s Energy, Housing and Utilities Sectors, includes vulnerable Ukrainian energy grid targets and even contemplates strikes against high-risk sites, including the switchyards at several nuclear power plants. But several European officials familiar with the document have said it is likely to have been produced by analytical departments linked to the Russian military or intelligence agencies, and that copies have circulated in Ukraine in previous years.
This year, however, Russia appears to be putting those plans into practice. On Tuesday night, Russian strikes hit three major power plants in the Kyiv region – Combined Heat and Power Plants 4 and 5, and the Trypillya Thermal Power Plant in the Kyiv region – as well as a major electrical substation that transfers electricity from two of the country’s main nuclear power plants to the capital. Those, along with other substations that make up the “Kyiv energy ring” that helps bring electricity from across the country to the capital, have all been targets of a Russian barrage that, if it succeeds, could threaten a humanitarian catastrophe in a city of 3 million people.
Ukraine’s energy minister first briefed European allies last month about the plan, a copy of which was first reported on by the New York Times. Russia has stepped up its attacks on the Ukrainian power grid precisely as Washington and Kyiv are embroiled in a major political quarrel. The Guardian is withholding some specifics of the military assessment at the request of Ukraine’s energy ministry, which said it could aid in further strikes against the country.
“There are two large documents, written very coldly and very clearly, with tables showing the number of weapons needed for each facility to destroy it,” Ukrainian energy minister Denys Shmyhal said last month. “The types of weapons range from ballistic missiles to jet-powered and conventional drones. We understand how they are going to act.”
The leaked documents identify bottlenecks and prescribe the number of ballistic and hypersonic cruise missiles and jet-powered drones needed to knock out power plants and substations with brutal efficiency. If that was done, it could destroy more than 90% of the power that Kyiv is budgeted for each winter, the document predicts, plunging the city into the dark and cold. A worst-case scenario could lead to blackouts lasting up to 20 hours a day until damaged equipment is repaired, according to expert projections.
The strikes on Ukraine’s energy system have become the country’s new frontlines – and the country is badly outgunned, as its stocks of US-provided PAC-3 missiles have dwindled. Zelenskyy this week castigated city authorities for poor planning for the winter and for failing to protect the city’s energy infrastructure. Protests over heat and electricity blackouts are also planned for Saturday, highlighting how the difficult conditions in winter could provoke a political crisis for the wartime government.
Economic Pravda, a leading Ukrainian news site that covers business, reported on Thursday that the three major power plants in Kyiv no longer have the ability to generate electricity, leaving the city more reliant on electricity brought from elsewhere in the country (the government generally does not confirm specific damage to critical infrastructure). The outlets’ experts predicted that, given a moderate winter and quick repairs of damaged substations, Kyiv residents could face up to eight hours of blackouts a day. Its worst case scenario of frigid temperatures and frequent attacks would leave Kyiv in the dark for up to 20 hours a day.
This is no accident for the Kremlin, which has increasingly turned to punishing Ukraine’s population in major cities as the frontlines of the conflict have frozen. “Last year, we were really on the brink,” said Volodymyr Fesenko, a Ukrainian political analyst. “But the risk this year will be even worse, so the plan [to attack Ukrainian infrastructure] does seem realistic. There’s a very high chance that Russia tries to break us this winter.” If the Kremlin believed in the chance of a clean military victory before now, he added, it may now believe that not to be possible, and focus on destroying Ukraine’s remaining infrastructure rather than trying to capture it.
The secret plan, which officials here say was intercepted by Ukrainian intelligence and then briefed to senior European diplomats by Shmyhal last month, sets itself several goals: to destroy electricity generation, heating, water and sewage systems in such a way as to to make life in Ukrainian cities unliveable. Destroying electrical generation alone, it says, would lead to a “significant decrease in the quality of life of the population.”
Graph showing rise in Russian attacks on Ukrainian heating infrastructure To do so, it specifies the number of weapons that should be allocated – Geran 4/5 jet-powered drones and hypersonic or cruise missiles – in order to destroy key infrastructure such as thermal power plants, substations, water pumping stations and more.
Considering strikes against the basis of Ukraine’s water, sewage and heating systems, the document says it would create a “large-scale municipal infrastructure crisis.”
When contacted by the Guardian, the Ukrainian Energy Ministry said that the document contained information that could aid in attacks on Ukrainian infrastructure. It did not reply to questions on how the current Russian bombing strategy matched with the proposals made in the document, or which Russian agency had developed the plans.
