### 23. ★★★☆☆ TSMC Says AI Chip Demand Is Still Far Above What It Can Supply. That's Why I'd Buy Nvidia Stock. - Yahoo Finance

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: GN AI
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-17T03:17:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: TSMC Says AI Chip Demand Is Still Far Above What It Can Supply. That's Why I'd Buy Nvidia Stock.&nbsp;&nbsp;Yahoo Finance

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

TSMC Says AI Chip Demand Is Still Far Above What It Can Supply. That's Why I'd Buy Nvidia Stock.
Nvidia (NASDAQ:NVDA) investors got a new look at the demand for artificial intelligence (AI) chips on Thursday, Oct. 15. Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing (NYSE:TSM), which makes chip wafers for Nvidia, posted third-quarter revenue of $46.7 billion -- over the high end of the $44.6 billion to $45.8 billion range management had forecast in July.
And TSMC's chairman and CEO, C.C. Wei, didn't sound worried about what's next.
Missed AI's "Act 1"? Act 2 Could Be 14x Bigger. Most investors think they missed the AI boat because they didn't buy Nvidia in 2005. But according to our analysts, we're only at the end of "Act 1"—the R&D phase. "Act 2" is the global rollout. Continue »
"[T]he demand actually is still far above our capability today to supply," Wei said on the company's earnings call. He made the comment in an answer where he wouldn't say if that demand comes more from AI accelerators or central processing units (CPUs).
Nvidia stock, though, headed the other way. Shares ended Thursday at $217.23, off around 9% from the record close they set on Oct. 6 ($239.24).
I think the gap between what Nvidia's supplier is reporting and what Nvidia's stock is doing makes the shares worth buying now.
Showing how much AI is powering the chipmaker, TSMC's revenue from high-performance computing (the platform that includes AI chips) climbed 22% sequentially in the third quarter. That was faster than the platform's 20% sequential growth in the second quarter. High-performance computing is now 68% of TSMC's revenue, up from 57% in the third quarter of 2025. Overall, TSMC's revenue grew 41% year over year in U.S. dollars. Net income , which TSMC reports in New Taiwan dollars, rose 69%.
Management expects more. TSMC guided for fourth-quarter revenue of $52.3 billion to $54.3 billion. The midpoint is around 14% over the third quarter's total, so the company is calling for another double-digit sequential gain after a 16% climb in the third quarter.
TSMC now sees 2026 revenue growing about 44% in U.S. dollars, up from its July outlook of a little over 40%.
Wei also pushed back on any idea that AI chip demand is cooling. He was asked about TSMC's forecast that its AI accelerator revenue would rise at an average yearly rate in the mid- to high-50% range from 2024 to 2029. Wei said "we did not see any sign of slowdown," adding that the rate "probably will be higher."
TSMC doesn't say how much of its revenue comes from Nvidia. So its report isn't a direct preview of Nvidia's next quarter.
But Nvidia sells chips on both sides of the split Wei wouldn't talk about. Its graphics processing units (GPUs) remain its main business, and last quarter it announced Vera, a CPU made for AI agents.
TSMC's fourth-quarter outlook covers October to December. And because chips take months to move from TSMC's wafers to finished Nvidia products, that forecast arguably says more about Nvidia's next few quarters than about the one ending this month.
And Nvidia's own results point the same way as TSMC's. Revenue rose to $96.2 billion in the second quarter of its fiscal 2027 (which ended July 26, 2026), up 18% from fiscal Q1 and 106% year over year. Management forecast around $108 billion in fiscal Q3, plus or minus 2%.
Of course, a shortage doesn't last forever. TSMC spent around $40.6 billion on capital expenditures through the first three quarters of 2026, and when supply eventually catches up to demand, Nvidia might lose some of its pricing power. But that doesn't look like a problem for the next few quarters.
At around $217, Nvidia's share price is about 14 times the earnings per share analysts expect for its next fiscal year (ending January 2028), on average. At the Oct. 6 record, it was around 15 times expected earnings. TSMC's shares, by comparison, go for about 19 times earnings, based on analysts' average forecast for 2027.
Put another way, investors are paying less for every dollar of Nvidia's expected profit than for TSMC's, even though Nvidia's revenue climbed more than twice as fast in each company's latest quarter. The market seems to doubt that Nvidia's growth can last.
Sure, that doubt might be right. If AI spending slows, Nvidia's sales could drop faster than those of a foundry with many kinds of customers. But TSMC's report doesn't show any sign of that yet.
In the end, I'd consider buying Nvidia stock at this price. I'd just keep the stake small. The stock is risky and volatile. After all, the shares dropped 9% in just seven trading days this month.
The Motley Fool Stock Advisor analyst team just identified what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy now… and Nvidia wasn't one of them. The 10 stocks that made the cut could produce monster returns in the coming years.
Consider when Netflix made this list on December 17, 2004... if you invested $1,000 at the time of our recommendation, you'd have $391,581 !* Or when Nvidia made this list on April 15, 2005... if you invested $1,000 at the time of our recommendation, you'd have $1,334,780 !*
Now, it's worth noting Stock Advisor's total average return is 936% — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 213% for the S&P 500. Don't miss the latest top 10 list, available with Stock Advisor , and join an investing community built by individual investors for individual investors.
Daniel Sparks and his clients do not have positions in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Nvidia and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy .
TSMC Says AI Chip Demand Is Still Far Above What It Can Supply. That's Why I'd Buy Nvidia Stock. was originally published by The Motley Fool

Ten days later, on 17 October that year, an Israeli strike hit the al-Muhandsee

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Wijdan Abu Matar, front, has managed to bring her children together in Cairo, Egypt, but her husband, Hatem, remains in Gaza. Photograph: Mohammed Salem/The Guardian
Wijdan Abu Matar, front, has managed to bring her children together in Cairo, Egypt, but her husband, Hatem, remains in Gaza. Photograph: Mohammed Salem/The Guardian
‘I cannot carry all of this alone’: the couples kept apart by the war in Gaza
Families have been scattered, fathers separated from their children, and couples unable to reunite and marry
Wijdan Abu Matar, 43, was forced to leave Gaza in November 2023 with her eight children for medical treatment, while her injured husband, Hatem, remained behind.
Wijdan and her family were living in the Nuseirat refugee camp when the Gaza war began with the 7 October 2023 Hamas attack on Israel. Ten days later, on 17 October that year, an Israeli strike hit the al-Muhandseen tower, near their home, injuring Wijdan and every member of her family.
“We were sitting together, just about to start lunch when the bombing happened,” Wijdan recalled. “The ceiling collapsed over our heads. Everything went dark. I couldn’t see anything. I just started screaming the names of my sons and daughters.”
Her daughter Nour, 15, suffered the most serious injuries, including a fractured skull and bleeding in the brain. Five-year-old Amna suffered severe burns to her chest. The family was initially separated after the strike.
Nour spent about 33 days in intensive care, with Wijdan by her side. Amna and Aysel, three and a half, were at a different hospital for 15 days.
Aysel, who incurred severe burns in the bombing, holds up a photograph of her father. Photograph: Mohammed Salem/The Guardian On 30 November 2023, Wijdan travelled with Nour to a hospital in Egypt for treatment. For months, the family remained scattered across Egypt, receiving medical treatment and staying with other relatives.
“I felt completely helpless,” Wijdan said. “There was a fire in my heart because I was away from my husband and my family was scattered.”
Eventually, Wijdan managed to bring her children together in an apartment in Egypt. Three years on, however, she is still separated from her husband, who remains in Gaza. “I have become both the mother and the father to my children,” she said. “The responsibility is exhausting me, and I am constantly worried about my husband.”
Hatem suffered a serious leg injury that limits his mobility and is now displaced in a tent in Nuseirat. Even speaking to him is difficult because of Gaza’s unreliable internet connection. “When he manages to get online, he calls me and the children. He tells me, ‘I am counting the days until I can see you again.’ I can see the tears in his eyes,” Wijdan says.
Speaking to Hatem is difficult because of Gaza’s unreliable internet connection, Wijdan says. Photograph: Mohammed Salem/The Guardian Wijdan has no regular source of income and relies on donations. Hatem stopped receiving a salary 10 months ago and his injury prevents him from working.
In Egypt, life is difficult for Wijdan and her family. She says she cannot afford transportation to send five of her children to school. “I see my children’s future slipping away in front of me, and there is nothing I can do.”
Nour still requires medical treatment, but Wijdan often cannot afford her medication. She sometimes considers returning to Gaza to be with Hatem, but fears exposing her children to further suffering.
“I dream of getting my children the treatment they need, and I hope we can reunite with my husband as soon as possible so he can also receive treatment,” Wijdan said. “I cannot carry all of this alone. Psychologically, I am exhausted. I have lost 35 members of my family in this war.”
‘Psychologically, I am exhausted. I have lost 35 members of my family in this war,’ says Wijdan Abu Matar. Photograph: Mohammed Salem/The Guardian Since the war began with Hamas’s attack on Israel that killed 1,200 people, at least 73,783 Palestinians have been killed and 174,738 wounded, according to the health ministry. Earlier this year an independent UN inquiry found that Israel continues to commit genocide by deliberately targeting Palestinian children in Gaza.
‘All I want to do is cry’: trauma of 7 October survivors three years after the attacks
Sami Abu Tabaq, a 29-year-old Palestinian photojournalist, was forced to leave the Gaza Strip in April 2024, leaving behind his family and his fiancee, Ola.
“Loneliness kills me every day,” he said. “Life away from the woman I love and my family is incredibly hard. I’m desperately waiting for the day God brings us together again.”
Sami became engaged to Ola five months before the war began. They had met by chance when he chose her family’s home as a location for a photoshoot. Before the war, they saw each other constantly. They went to the sea, had dinner at restaurants in Gaza, and spent months preparing the house where they planned to live after their wedding, which was scheduled for November 2023.
Sami and his family fled their home in Tal al-Zaatar, Jabaliya, early on in the war. Their home was badly damaged and later destroyed, along with the house Sami and Ola had been planning to move into once they got married.
When his sister decided to flee south with her children, Sami accompanied her but he could not reach Ola to say goodbye.
The separation became unbearable. Days, sometimes weeks, passed without Sami knowing whether Ola was alive. Once, he saw reports that an entire residential block near her home in Gaza City had been bombed. “I almost lost my mind. I had never felt so helpless,” he said. Unable to return north, he asked his brother to check on her. Eventually, his brother confirmed that she and her family were alive.
‘I couldn’t cry over my children like everyone else’: the tragedy of Palestinian journalist Wael al-Dahdouh
Sami continued working as a photojournalist from southern Gaza. Four journalists who had shared his tent were later killed, including Hamza al-Dahdouh, a son of the Al Jazeera journalist Wael al-Dahdouh.
As Israel threatened a ground invasion of Rafah in 2024, Ola began urging Sami to leave Gaza, fearing he would be killed. Sami paid a company coordinating exits at the time for both of them to cross into Egypt. But Ola could not reach southern Gaza to travel through Rafah. “She told me, ‘You go, and I’ll follow you,’” Sami said. “And here we are, still waiting, nearly two and a half years later.” They still have not managed to get married.
By his second day in Egypt, Sami was overwhelmed by guilt. “The overthinking kills me,” he said. “I felt ashamed to turn on the lights or eat proper food while my fiancee and my family were struggling to find the most basic things.”
In Egypt, his life has become dominated by loneliness and sleeplessness. “There is safety in Egypt and there are beautiful places, but I stay home. Happiness has no meaning without her. I cannot enjoy anything while she is not here,” he said.
Sami Abu Tabaq sits alone in his apartment in Cairo, while his fiancee, Ola, remains in Gaza. Images of Gaza are shown on the television. Photograph: Mohammed Salem/The Guardian Despite the war, Ola completed her bachelor’s degree in nursing at the Islamic University of Gaza. She has also endured enormous loss: her cousin, the journalist Ayat Khadoura , was killed, along with about 50 other members of her extended family.
From Egypt, Sami tries to create moments of happiness for her. For her latest birthday, he contacted her brother in Gaza and secretly arranged a cake, gift and flowers. “When she woke up and saw everything, she was so happy. I love her reaction when she’s happy.” The only problem was the flowers. “They were artificial,” he said. “I wasn’t happy with them, but natural flowers can’t get into Gaza because Israel does not allow them in.”
Sami says he has tried every possible way to get Ola out, without success. She is now applying for scholarships abroad, hoping education might provide a route to evacuation and, eventually, to him.
Sami recently travelled to Mecca for Umrah, where one prayer remained constantly on his mind. “I prayed, ‘God, please bring us together soon,’” he said. “But one thing I know for sure: I will wait for her for the rest of my life, until God brings us together.”

</details>

- **发生了什么**: Trump pauses new Russia sanctions passed by Congress amid deepening row with Ukraine&nbsp;&nbsp;The Guardian

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Donald Trump says he has waived many ⁠sanctions, restrictions and duties ⁠on Russia. Photograph: Eric Lee/Getty Images
Donald Trump says he has waived many ⁠sanctions, restrictions and duties ⁠on Russia. Photograph: Eric Lee/Getty Images
Trump pauses new Russia sanctions passed by Congress amid deepening row with Ukraine
President signed the legislation championed by late senator Lindsey Graham last month but has waived the restrictions as they were due to come into effect
Donald Trump has put on hold a sweeping new sanctions package targeting the Russian economy, telling Congress the pause on the legislation passed by both chambers was in the national interest.
In an executive order on Friday, the US president said he was waiving many ⁠sanctions, restrictions and duties ⁠on Russia, in a move that comes as he has been publicly criticising ⁠Ukrainian president Volodymyr Zelenskyy, including suggesting that Ukraine needs a ​new leader.
The move was greeted by Russian presidential envoy Kirill Dmitriev, who wrote on social media: “The Trump administration’s suspension of the Graham Act is an important and truly historic moment.”
Trump signed the legislation last month, after Congress passed the measure in an act of rare bipartisan agreement. It sought to clamp down more quickly on the flow of financial resources fuelling Russia’s war against Ukraine.
Power plant attacks reflect Russian blueprint to make Ukrainian cities unlivable
The legislation – whose ‌ primary backer in ‌Congress was the late senator Lindsey Graham – sanctions Russian officials, banks and a shadow fleet of tankers that keeps Russian energy moving and was due to be implemented in two days.
It also directs Trump to impose up to 100% tariffs on the top five importers of Russian oil or natural gas, with an exception for countries that import less than 15% of Russia’s natural gas exports and have taken significant steps to reduce those imports.
Friday’s announcement comes a week after Trump said the US had agreed to purchase diesel from Russia to try to bring down global energy prices, upending years of US and western pressure on the Kremlin to end its war against Ukraine.
Trump said last week that Russia would immediately supply more than 300,000 metric tons of diesel, followed by an additional 500,000 tons in November and 1m tons “immediately thereafter”. Russia would deliver an additional 3m tons “within a short period of time” after that, he wrote.
The president has face widespread criticism after making the announcement, with a dozen US senators – including six Republicans – calling on Trump ⁠to rescind his diesel deal.
A letter from the bipartisan group of lawmakers sent on Tuesday urged Trump to execute the sanctions authorities included in the legislation passed last month, called the “Lindsey O. Graham Sanctioning Russia and Iran Act of 2026”.
Under the legislation he signed into law, Trump had until 18 October to impose the sanctions and tariffs on Russian energy, or waive them. The administration had not told lawmakers whether it would impose the sanctions or issue a waiver.
Zelenskyy said on Friday the diesel deal would mean cash “will start flowing back into Russia”, as he met European leaders in Brussels .
Trump ⁠blindsided Zelenskyy last weekend by announcing that Ukraine and Russia had agreed not to target each other’s energy facilities. Facing midterm elections next month, Trump has blamed Ukrainian strikes on Russian refineries for ​pushing up energy prices that could cost his Republican party its thin majorities in the US ‌Senate and House of Representatives.
Trump’s administration is suspected of withholding intelligence from Ukraine on incoming Russian ballistic missile strikes, and of throttling Starshield, the military version of the Starlink satellite internet network, to prevent it from being used on drones flying faster than 100mph (161km/h). Neither has officially been confirmed.
The US sanctions legislation has also become a campaign centerpiece for Senator Darline Graham, Lindsey Graham’s sister appointed to serve out the remaining months of his term, who is also running to replace him in the Senate more permanently.
In a debate earlier this week with South Carolina Democratic nominee Annie Andrews, Graham – who has Trump’s endorsement and is expected to campaign with him in South Carolina next week – said she had been “so proud” to help get the measure passed but acknowledged it was Trump’s purview as to how he implemented it.
“It gives the president tools that he can use, if he so chooses, to end the war through economic sanctions,” Graham said during the debate in Columbia. “So that’s up to him as to whether or not he wants to use it, or whether he wants to use something else, but I think the purpose of it is to bring down the gas prices and ultimately end the war. And when the war ends, gas prices will fall.”

### 26. ★★★☆☆ No 10 leaves option open to impose Chinese EV tariffs if UK joins EU scheme - The Guardian

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: GN China / Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T19:05:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: No 10 leaves option open to impose Chinese EV tariffs if UK joins EU scheme&nbsp;&nbsp;The Guardian

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

An electric vehicle production line in Ningbo, China. Photograph: Nick Carey/Reuters
An electric vehicle production line in Ningbo, China. Photograph: Nick Carey/Reuters
No 10 leaves option open to impose Chinese EV tariffs if UK joins EU scheme
Beijing warns against ‘discriminatory’ step after France drops opposition to British role in Made in Europe package
No 10 has left the door open to imposing tariffs on Chinese-made electric vehicles as part of the Made in Europe package, prompting a warning from Beijing that such a move would be “discriminatory”.
Downing Street said on Friday that it would “welcome the objective to have the UK included in the Industrial Accelerator Act” after France dropped its opposition to Britain joining the scheme .
The aim of the rules is to curb subsidised Chinese imports by giving EU-made products preferential treatment, but the UK and others such as Norway are worried about being left at a disadvantage if excluded from the scheme.
The price for joining would be aligning with the EU’s tariffs on Chinese EVs imposed by the bloc in 2024, according to a Franco-German proposal on Thursday night that said the UK would have to dynamically align with EU trade defence measures.
The Chinese embassy in London said it was “firmly opposed to any discriminatory practice involving tariff hikes or restrictive measures on Chinese products”.
“We have expressed serious concern to the UK side regarding the reports in question,” it said, adding that “trade protectionism runs counter to WTO rules and market principles”.
This week, the business secretary, Jonathan Reynolds, said he would not be coerced into aligning with the EU’s policy.
However, the government has been desperate to be included in the Made in Europe package, fearing billions of pounds of trade in the car industry could be lost if the UK is locked out of supply chains in the EU.
Asked whether the UK accepted it would have to put tariffs on Chinese-made vehicles in order to join Made in Europe, a No 10 spokesperson said it was “premature to speculate” on the terms of any deal with the bloc and there was “still a European process to run”.
They added: “But more broadly, on the principle, the UK obviously has its own trade policy and it keeps all tariffs under review. And we work closely with the British automotive sector to make sure that that approach reflects their best interests.”
Chinese EVs are increasingly popular, with BYD sales up 78% year on year in September, Chery up 660% and the lesser-known Changan up by more than 3,000% in terms of market share.
EU lawmakers are expected to finalise the Industrial Accelerator Act, known as Made in Europe legislation, by the end of the year or early 2027.
But MEPs have already tabled 5,100 amendments, including one embracing the UK and giving Andy Burnham time to secure the deal he wants.
Earlier this week the boss of Mercedes-Benz, Ola Källenius, who is also the president of the European car trade body ACEA, warned that locking the UK out of legislation would amount to a “deliberate choice” by EU leaders to “hurt” the car industry across the bloc.
According to the new Franco-German proposal, there would be “an opt-in to further include European partners who were part of the EU single market as of 1 January 2020”, which includes the UK and Norway.
This would be provided those third countries accepted “dynamic alignment on the EU’s conditions related to third countries’ investments and on relevant EU trade defence measures”.
Tariffs could help UK-based manufacturers by preventing cut-price Chinese models flooding the market but they could also push up prices for consumers and harm British manufacturers trying to sell vehicles to China in the case of an escalating trade war. About 80% of UK-made cars are exported, with more than half going to the EU and 6% to China.
Earlier on Thursday the trade minister, Anas Sarwar, hinted that the UK was minded to align on tariffs. He told Politico the UK and the EU must “work together and act in lockstep” to tackle China’s dominance in the automotive sector.
A car industry source said the EU “has recently shown that it is possible to have a dialogue with the Chinese and get some form of safeguarding in place” without retaliation.
China opposes the expansion of EV tariffs. In the past few months it has made what European Commission officials called “bloodcurdling threats” to launch a trade war over the latest plan to impose quotas on Chinese hybrid cars.
While quotas can be introduced within months under World Trade Organization rules, it could take more than a year to impose tariffs on Chinese cars, with strict processes to be followed, starting with notification of an investigation and followed by evidence-gathering and sharing over alleged harms China might be causing to the British car industry.
Liam Byrne, the Labour chair of the parliamentary business committee, has said imposing tariffs on Chinese EVs would be “a small price to pay” to save the industry in both the UK and the EU.
At a conference in London on Thursday night, he said the UK “urgently needs” a new strategy on China to ensure that British and European manufacturing “does not die”.
Byrne, who has been leading an investigation into the UK’s position on China, was highly critical of the government’s approach, which he believes is naive and based on false hopes that China will change its export policy.
“Our China strategy is simply not where it needs to be. The UK urgently needs a coherent China strategy, which it does not have today,” he said.

### 27. ★★★☆☆ ‘Tough to watch, impossible to ignore’: reviewers on NAZA documentary

- **发生了什么**: Film interviewing military insiders about mass killing of civilians in Gaza wins widespread praise around the worldCritics around the world have hailed the award-winning documentary NAZA as a “brilliant and harrowing” film that is “impossible to ignore”.NAZA, which is produced by the Guardian and details Israel’s mass killing of civilians in Gaza, was released in UK cinemas this week and will be shown in more than 50 countries. As

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

A scene from NAZA, which interviewed military insiders anonymously on the rooftops of Tel Aviv at night. Photograph: NAZA/The Guardian
A scene from NAZA, which interviewed military insiders anonymously on the rooftops of Tel Aviv at night. Photograph: NAZA/The Guardian
‘Tough to watch, impossible to ignore’: reviewers on NAZA documentary
Film interviewing military insiders about mass killing of civilians in Gaza wins widespread praise around the world
Critics around the world have hailed the award-winning documentary NAZA as a “brilliant and harrowing” film that is “impossible to ignore”.
NAZA, which is produced by the Guardian and details Israel’s mass killing of civilians in Gaza, was released in UK cinemas this week and will be shown in more than 50 countries. As well as its global cinema releases, NAZA will be available free on the Guardian’s website from late November.
The investigative documentary, which contains 24 interviews with Israeli military insiders speaking anonymously on the rooftops of Tel Aviv at night, has received widespread praise.
In a five-star review, the Financial Times’s Danny Leigh says that as a piece of cinema, “journalism and film-making brilliantly entwine”. The Times calls the film “hugely provocative and a disturbing account” of Israel’s mass killing of civilians in Gaza, and the Irish Times brands NAZA an “essential record of engineered nightmare”. In a four-star review, the Guardian’s Peter Bradshaw says the “ anguish of Gaza is brought into pin-sharp focus ”.
British Vogue calls NAZA a “brilliant and harrowing film” that outlines the “restrained, meticulous, bone-chilling depiction of the progressive erasure of Gaza”. The magazine says the film “feels destined to become an enduring historical document, one which will and should be seen by generations to come”.
Time Out magazine says NAZA “puts together a restrained, intelligent, articulate, precise and utterly damning case against Israel’s actions in Gaza and their ongoing actions through the region. As a work of cinema, it is exemplary. As a work of journalism, it is inarguable. As a document, it is an indictment.”
The New York Times makes the film a “critic’s choice” and calls NAZA a “haunting” documentary that is “tough to watch, and impossible to ignore”. The Los Angeles Times says NAZA is a “stone-cold frightening” film that “burns the conscience”. And in New York magazine, it is described as “one of the most important films of our time”.
NAZA: the film Israel does not want you to see - video
The Hollywood Reporter praises NAZA as a “sober and thoroughly documented bombshell of an exposé” that “leaves one with a deadening feeling, as if humanity may have lost all hope”. The Toronto Star says “NAZA is heartbreaking but essential viewing, questioning the systems that cause and quantify civilian death in modern warfare”.
The Spanish outlet El Mundo hailed NAZA as “the film of our time, a superb, electrifying, and devastating documentary about the systematic model of annihilation of the Palestinian people” that ultimately “points the finger at us. At night, in silence.”
The French newspaper Le Monde says the film “has the effect of an uppercut that leaves the viewer stunned”, while the Danish culture magazine Soundvenue calls NAZA a “soul-crushing testimony to the genocide in Gaza” that is an “example of how the world increasingly seems like a dystopia”.
The United Arab Emirates’ state-owned newspaper the National calls NAZA a “sickeningly tough film to watch”, and an editorial in the Israeli-outlet Haaretz says the film is “trying to force Israelis to confront what they already know: that Israel’s conduct of the war in Gaza has left a black stain”.
When the film opened in Italy last month, it topped the charts . The Italian newspaper Corriere della Sera says NAZA lasts “only 80 minutes, but no more is needed: the ‘scientific’ coldness with which the interviewees reconstruct how they are destroying Gaza is something unforgettable”.
The film contains 24 interviews with Israeli military insiders who detail how an AI-assisted apparatus of mass surveillance was activated during the Gaza war, revealing the systems that were used to target Hamas operatives in the knowledge that large numbers of Palestinian civilians would also die.
Various organisations, including the UN, Amnesty International, B’Tselem and Physicians for Human Rights, the latter two of which are Israeli-based, have said Israel is committing genocide against Palestinians in Gaza.
The film premiered at the Venice film festival, where it was received a 25-minute standing ovation – a festival record. The documentary went on to win the special jury prize .
NAZA: Netanyahu orders identification and prosecution of film’s interviewees
NAZA film-makers say they spoke to more than 100 sources about Israeli actions in Gaza
Gaza documentary NAZA beats Avengers Endgame: Encore to top Italian box office
Why is Israel so afraid of the film NAZA? We spoke to its directors – Stateside with Kai and Carter
NAZA film-makers address threats at documentary’s New York City premiere
Global release announced for acclaimed Gaza documentary NAZA
From ovations in Venice to death threats in Israel: the extreme responses to NAZA documentary
‘They know the house they are bombing is full of children’: NAZA’s directors on their devastating film about Israeli intelligence

Fed Chair Kevin Warsh warns of AI 'shock' for economy, pledges to contain inflation
Warsh said AI would ultimately generate wealth but the path may be "very bumpy."
Federal Reserve Chair Kevin Warsh warned of a potential "shock" to the U.S. economy delivered by a rise in artificial intelligence spending , pledging to contain price increases as a flood of private sector funds drives up demand.
"If you’re a central banker, you’re in the business of trying to ensure stable prices and full employment, you’ve got to recognize this as a shock," Warsh said in Bangkok, Thailand, at a conference organized by the International Monetary Fund.
A surge in AI spending has helped boost the U.S. economy in recent quarters. In theory, an onrush of investment in activities like chip manufacturing and data center construction could put upward pressure in general on consumer prices, unless the demand is offset by a new supply of goods and services.
Which grocery prices are rising the fastest and which are falling?
"It’s obviously hitting the demand side of the economy before it’s hitting the supply side of the economy," Warsh said.
Warsh, who took the helm of the Fed in May, has previously weighed in on the potential impact of AI on the labor market and wider economy.
On Friday, Warsh reiterated his optimism about AI, saying the technology would ultimately generate added productivity and wealth.
But, Warsh added, "the path from where we are to there is likely to be a very bumpy one."
"We really don’t know what the market structure will be of these new technologies," Warsh said. "Who&#x27;s going to be making the money?"
Warsh acknowledged other shocks faced by the U.S. economy, including "shocks of war and military conflict."
The Nscale data center project in Barstow, Texas, Oct. 15, 2026.
The outbreak of the Iran war in February imposed a historic oil supply shock and caused an acceleration of price increases in many countries. A U.S. government report issued this week showed inflation had climbed to a 4-month high in September as the Middle East conflict continued to drive up gasoline prices.
Prices rose 3.7% in September, according to new data from the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics .

Inflation stands more than a percentage point higher than the Fed&#x27;s target rate of 2%.
"It’s hard to be in a discussion with central bankers and not compare notes and hear the same thing about the surge in prices," Warsh said on Friday.
He later added: "I’ve heard resolve from my colleagues around the world to deliver price stability. I’ve certainly delivered that resolve."
Price hikes prompted the Fed to raise interest rates a quarter of a percentage point in September, marking the central bank&#x27;s first rate increase since 2023.
An increase to the benchmark interest rate aims to slash inflation by slowing the economy and eating away at demand, though the policy typically yields results after a lag time lasting several months.
The next meeting of Fed policymakers takes place on Oct. 28, just days before the midterm elections.
Gas prices set record for highest number of days above $4 a gallon in a calendar year
Consistent with his approach in previous public appearances, Warsh opted against issuing a forecast about what the central bank may do at its meeting later this month.
"I don’t pre-judge meetings. I don’t believe in saying now what I might do at the meetings we have over the balance of this year," Warsh said.
Many investors appeared to interpret Warsh&#x27;s posture as a sign the Fed may hold interest rates steady later this month.
As of Friday morning, investors peg the chance of the Fed maintaining current interest rates at about 86%, which marks an uptick from about 55% on Wednesday, according to CME Group’s FedWatch Tool , a measure of market sentiment.

### 36. ★★★☆☆ Not Nvidia. Not TSMC. This Equipment Maker Is the Real Bottleneck in AI Chip Production. - The Motley Fool

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: GN AI
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T19:55:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Not Nvidia. Not TSMC. This Equipment Maker Is the Real Bottleneck in AI Chip Production.&nbsp;&nbsp;The Motley Fool

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Nvidia ( NVDA -1.43% ) and TSMC ( TSM -2.28% ) are often considered the bellwethers of the AI chip market. Nvidia , the world&#x27;s largest producer of discrete GPUs for data centers, provides the best "picks and shovels" for the AI gold rush. TSMC , the world&#x27;s largest and most advanced contract chipmaker, manufactures Nvidia&#x27;s chips. It also serves other fabless AI chipmakers, including AMD ( AMD -5.96% ) and Broadcom ( AVGO -4.53% ) .
Therefore, if Nvidia stopped developing new AI chips and TSMC stopped manufacturing them, the AI market would grind to a halt. However, neither company could produce new chips without ASML ( ASML -0.96% ) -- the real bottleneck in the AI supply chain.
Why is ASML the "real" bottleneck in the AI chip market?
ASML, which is based in the Netherlands, is the world&#x27;s largest producer of lithography systems. These systems enable chip foundries, like TSMC, to optically etch circuit patterns onto silicon wafers. It dominates the market for lower-end deep-ultraviolet (DUV) lithography systems, which are used to produce older, larger chips. It&#x27;s also the only producer of high-end extreme ultraviolet (EUV) systems, which are used to produce the world&#x27;s smallest and densest chips.
ASML began developing its first EUV systems nearly three decades ago, giving it a major first-mover advantage against its industry peers. These massive systems cost between $200 million and $400 million, require multiple planes to ship, and must be assembled on-site.
Market cap Market cap calculated using publicly traded shares outstanding only. Does not include unlisted, private, or dual-class non-traded shares. Implied market cap may vary. $632.67B
All of the world&#x27;s leading foundries -- including TSMC, Samsung, and Intel ( INTC -5.79% ) -- use ASML&#x27;s EUV systems to manufacture their newest chips. Therefore, the latest AI chips from Nvidia, AMD, and Broadcom -- as well as high-bandwidth memory (HBM) chips for AI clusters -- couldn&#x27;t exist without ASML&#x27;s EUV machines.
ASML doesn&#x27;t face any competitors in this market because it would cost too much to develop and mass-produce new EUV systems. That&#x27;s why ASML still has plenty of room to raise its prices (and expand its gross margins) without worrying about losing any customers.
In the past, ASML generated more revenue from DUV systems than from EUV systems. But over the past three years, its EUV revenue has overtaken its DUV revenue as more foundries upgraded their systems to produce smaller, denser, and more power-efficient AI chips.
In the first half of 2026, ASML generated more than half of its revenue from its EUV systems (including its older "low-NA" systems and newer "high-NA" systems). That percentage should continue to rise as Nvidia and its peers launch even more advanced chips, and that growth should offset its declining DUV shipments -- which were exacerbated by tighter restrictions on its deliveries of higher-end DUV systems to Chinese chipmakers.
For 2026, ASML expects its revenue to rise 35%-38% with a gross margin of 56%. It&#x27;s already raised that outlook twice in the second and third quarters of 2026, compared to its original guidance for 22% revenue growth with a gross margin of 51%-53% at the start of the year.
Analysts expect its revenue and EPS to rise 36% and 68%, respectively, for the full year. From 2026 to 2028, they expect its revenue and EPS to grow at CAGRs of 26% and 36%, respectively. Its stock still looks reasonably valued at 26 times next year&#x27;s earnings, even though it&#x27;s already rallied 177% over the past three years.
Nvidia and TSMC are still great AI chip plays, but they have clear weaknesses. Nvidia faces intense competition from AMD&#x27;s cheaper GPUs, Broadcom&#x27;s custom AI accelerators for hyperscalers, and other inference-oriented chipmakers. TSMC is constantly increasing its capex to ramp up its production and meet the market&#x27;s insatiable demand for new AI chips.
So if you want a balanced way to invest in the AI market without betting on a single chipmaker or fretting over a contract chipmaker&#x27;s rising capex, ASML checks all the right boxes. It controls a crucial link in the AI supply chain, is growing rapidly, and has a massive moat.

Stock Market Today: Dow, S&P 500 Futures Rise as Bessent Intensifies Economic Pressure on Iran, Treasury Yields Cool Off — TSLA, AA, IBKR in Focus
U.S. stock futures were higher on Friday, as the Dow Jones, S&P 500 and Nasdaq 100 indices rose, following Thursday’s higher close.
Treasury yields pulled back from multi-decade highs Thursday, with a rout in semiconductor stocks sending investors into government bonds, according to The Kobeissi Letter. The 10-year Treasury yield traded around 5.085% Thursday, down from a 24-year high of 5.36% last week, while the 30-year was at 5.429%, down from 5.73%, its highest level since 2002.
Separately, U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said Washington would intensify economic pressure on Iran , writing that “The noose is tightening around Iran’s economy,” as a naval blockade curbs oil exports and the rial tumbles; Reuters reported Iran’s seaborne crude exports fell to zero in the second week of September and remained at zero as of Oct. 5.

Fed holds interest rates steady as inflation hits 3-year high
Futures markets overwhelmingly expected the Fed to keep rates unchanged.
The Federal Reserve held interest rates steady on Wednesday as the economy weathers its highest inflation in three years.
The announcement marked the first decision on interest rates rates since Trump nominee Kevin Warsh took the helm as Fed chair.
Speaking at a press conference, Warsh voiced a commitment to bring inflation down to the Fed&#x27;s desired level of 2%. The annual pace of price increases currently registers at more than twice that target rate.
"Persistently high prices are a burden for the American people," Warsh told reporters in Washington, D.C. "This committee will deliver price stability."
All 12 members of the FOMC voted to hold interest rates steady, meaning Warsh and his predecessor, former Fed Chair Jerome Powell, agreed on the rate decision.
Nine members indicated they expect to raise interest rates by the end of the year, marking a significant change in sentiment from a previous projection issued three months earlier.
Iran live updates: Trump, Vance were both signatories on MOU
The Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC), a 12-member policymaking body at the Fed, issued a statement describing inflation as "elevated."
The current level of inflation in part reflects "supply shocks that have driven price increases in certain sectors, including energy," the FOMC said.
The policy move also arrived at a moment of flux for the nation’s economy, just days after an agreement between the United States and Iran offered hope for some price relief.
The U.S.-Iran accord, set to be formally signed on Friday, came as gasoline prices fell below $4 a gallon for the first time since March. Still, fuel costs stand well above pre-war levels, and an array of grocery prices remain elevated.
In recent weeks, odds have risen for a potential interest rate hike by the end of 2026, according to the CME FedWatch Tool, which grants a roughly four in 10 chance of a quarter-point increase in December.
The shift in expectations came after a stronger-than-expected jobs report earlier this month showed robust hiring in May. In theory, a resilient labor market could afford central bankers leeway to raise interest rates in an effort to dial back inflation, since elevated borrowing costs risk a hiring slowdown.
Bend, Oregon, shooting: Suspect ID&#x27;d, had attended elementary school, police say
Iran live updates: Trump admin prepares options, including &#x27;mother of all bombs&#x27;
Hurricane Isaias: At least 4 dead as Isaias downgraded
Inflation jumped for a third consecutive month as the Iran war continued to drive up prices in May, surpassing 4% for the first time in three years
Federal Reserve Chair Kevin Warsh delivers a speech on the day of his swearing-in ceremony, in the East Room of the White House in Washington, May 22, 2026.
The Middle East conflict prompted the Iranian closure of the Strait of Hormuz , a maritime trading route that facilitates the transport of about one-fifth of global oil supply. The standoff triggered one of the largest oil shocks ever recorded, sending gasoline prices surging.
On Monday, President Donald Trump announced a U.S.-Iran deal that included plans to reopen the strait. Iranian Deputy Foreign Minister Kazem Gharibabadi confirmed the deal had been finalized and said it would be signed in Switzerland on Friday. Oil prices fell to their lowest level since March.
The benchmark rate stands at a level between 3.5% and 3.75%. That figure marks a significant drop from a recent peak attained in 2023, but borrowing costs remain well above a 0% rate established at the outset of the COVID-19 pandemic.
The rate decision is the first major policy move overseen by Warsh. During his term as a Fed governor in the late 2000s and early 2010s, Warsh gained a reputation as an interest-rate "hawk," meaning he generally preferred higher interest rates as a means of ensuring low and stable inflation.
Last year, Warsh voiced support for lower interest rates. At his Senate confirmation hearing in April, Warsh emphasized the threat posed by elevated inflation.
"When inflation surges -- as it has done in recent years -- grievous harm is done to our citizens, especially to the least well-off," Warsh said.
Bucking typical norms, Powell casted a vote on interest rates as a member of the Fed&#x27;s policymaking board.
Oil prices fall to lowest level since March after US announces Iran deal
Powell said he would stay on at the central bank&#x27;s board of governors after his term as chair expired as an investigation into the Fed&#x27;s office renovation continues.
The Department of Justice moved to drop a criminal probe into Powell in April, calling on the Fed&#x27;s inspector general to carry out the investigation into cost overruns tied to the renovation. Powell will remain on the Fed&#x27;s board for an indeterminate length of time, he said last month.
The criminal investigation into Powell focused on alleged false testimony to Congress about an office renovation. Powell, who was appointed by Trump in 2017, has rebuked the probe as a politically motivated effort to influence interest-rate policy. Trump denied any involvement in the criminal investigation.

### 44. ★★★☆☆ The Israeli politicians seeking to replace Netanyahu

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-17T05:00:58+00:00
- **发生了什么**: A look at Israel&#039;s main opposition leaders, their parties and their largely shared positions on Gaza and other wars.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Share The Israeli politicians seeking to replace Netanyahu on social media
Do any of these Israeli candidates support a Palestinian state?
On September 26, five leading members of Israel’s self-styled Zionist opposition signed a non-aggression pact, seeking to oust incumbent Benjamin Netanyahu in the October 27 election.
Gathered at the Tel Aviv home of opposition leader Yair Lapid were his co-runner, former Prime Minister Naftali Bennett, leader of Beyachad; former Chief of Staff Gadi Eisenkot, leader of the new Yashar party; Yair Golan, leader of the Democrats party; and Avigdor Liberman, head of the Yisrael Beiteinu party.
list of 4 items list 1 of 4 How genocidal war on Gaza is shaping Israel’s election
list 2 of 4 Despite long history with Netanyahu, Trump hedges as Israel vote nears
list 3 of 4 Israeli opposition signal foreign policy change in style, but not substance
list 4 of 4 If Netanyahu goes, does anything really change for Palestinians?
In the statement of principles published after the hours-long meeting, the leaders said, “Despite ideological differences among us, the formation of a Zionist, statesmanlike government dedicated to repair and reconstruction is our shared overarching goal.”
However, how long that pact might last — Liberman has already accused frontrunner Eisenkot of seeking a deal with Netanyahu — how meaningful a change in substance as well as style they offer, and which of them might lead a future coalition, remain open to question .
From left, Israeli opposition leaders Yair Lapid, Gadi Eisenkot, Yair Golan, Naftali Bennett and Avigdor Lieberman meet on September 26, 2026 [Courtesy of Yair Lapid via X] With the exception of Golan, all take a fundamentally right-wing approach to Israel’s challenges.
None is committed to establishing a Palestinian state, with only the Democrats acknowledging it as a distant, unspecified goal. None, Golan included, accepts that Israel’s war on Gaza amounts to genocide or that it has committed war crimes in killing more than 74,000 people there. Nor has any questioned Israeli military actions against Lebanon, Syria, Yemen, Qatar and Iran, instead reserving criticism mostly for how those actions have allowed Israel to be perceived internationally.
“The dominant forces in the opposition such as Bennett, Liberman or Eisenkot represent right-wing politics,” said Ahron Bregman, an Israeli analyst and senior teaching fellow at King’s College London. “They are not willing to touch the real, fundamental issue which is at the heart of everything: the Palestinian issue, and the Palestinian aspirations for independence.” He likened them to Netanyahu, but without the corruption allegations and support of far-right figures, such as National Security Minister Itamar Ben-Gvir and Finance Minister Bezalel Smotrich. “Israel as a whole is a right-wing nation,” he added.
So, who are the main opposition figures and what do their parties stand for?
Gadi Eisenkot, leader of the Yashar party, delivers a speech [File: Erik Marmor/Getty Images] Gadi Eisenkot – Yashar
Credited with originating the Dahiyeh Doctrine in 2008, involving “ disproportionate force ” and large-scale destruction of civilian infrastructure against groups such as Hezbollah and Hamas, Eisenkot served as Israeli military chief from 2015 to 2019.
The 66-year-old entered politics in 2022 with former Defence Minister Benny Gantz’s National Unity alliance and later joined the war cabinet assembled by Netanyahu after the Hamas-led attacks in southern Israel on October 7, 2023. In December that year, his son Gal, 25, and two nephews were killed while participating in the genocide in Gaza, losses commentators say have helped forge a connection with an electorate that has come to see itself as scarred by war.
In June 2024, he quit the war cabinet alongside Gantz , accusing Netanyahu of lacking a strategy to end the war. A year later, Eisenkot founded the centrist Yashar (Straight/Honest), advocating for professional government, military service for all – including ultra-Orthodox Jews – and a state commission of inquiry into the failings before October 7.
While notoriously ambiguous on many of Israel’s foreign policy positions, he has been critical of the government’s reliance upon the United States in achieving its war aims, while not questioning those aims themselves. In June, he characterised Washington’s mediated negotiations with Tehran to end the war against Iran that Washington had embarked upon with Israel as “a deal taking shape far from here and far from Israeli interests” and, the same month, railed against US restrictions on Israel’s freedom of action in Lebanon.
Eisenkot has ruled out including the United Arab List, or Ra’am party, in a post-election coalition. In a September 26 Channel 12 interview , he said no Arab party would accept his conditions, including recognition of Israel as a Jewish state.
Former Israeli Prime Ministers Naftali Bennett, left, and Yair Lapid, right [File: Abir Sultan/EPA] Naftali Bennett, Yair Lapid – Beyachad
Beyachad (Together) is the joint list announced in April 2026 by former Prime Ministers Naftali Bennett and Yair Lapid, who served for a combined 18 months in 2021-2022 between Netanyahu governments.
Bennett, a 54-year-old longtime supporter of illegal settlements in the occupied West Bank, leads the list, with Lapid, the 62-year-old founder of the centrist Yesh Atid party, in second place.
Lapid has called his partner “a right-wing politician, but an honest one”. Neither has prioritised resolving the Israeli-Palestinian conflict or supported a Palestinian state. Though their 2021 coalition included Ra’am, Bennett has acknowledged that this broke an electoral promise.
Lapid has been one of Israel’s most outspoken critics of how its genocide and multiple strikes upon its neighbours are perceived internationally, while rarely questioning the rationale behind those actions. Bennett, in contrast, is more outspoken in his hawkish views. A full-throated supporter of the country’s war on Iran, he also congratulated Israel’s military and security services for a September 2025 attack on Qatar’s capital, Doha, that targeted senior Hamas leaders as they met to consider a Gaza ceasefire proposal put forth by the US. A Qatari security official was killed in the attack, which drew condemnation from across the region and beyond.
They now seek a government composed solely of Zionist parties, also ruling out reliance on Arab parties.
Avigdor Lieberman speaks at a Yisrael Beiteinu party meeting [File: Amir Levy/Getty Images] Avigdor Lieberman – Yisrael Beiteinu
The most hawkish voice at Lapid’s table, Lieberman, 68, founded Yisrael Beiteinu (Israel Our Home) in 1999 to represent right-leaning Russian-speaking immigrants.
Once Netanyahu’s protege, Lieberman has ruled out serving under him or joining a coalition that includes the ultra-Orthodox or relies on Arab parties, which he calls anti-Zionist. He has also rejected a minority government, reportedly making him a principal obstacle to Eisenkot becoming the bloc’s prime ministerial candidate.
In November 2023, Lieberman repeated his 2018 assertion, made then as defence minister, that there were “no innocents in Gaza”. In February, he visited the Karem Abu Salem crossing, known as Kerem Shalom to Israelis, and accused Netanyahu’s government of funding Hamas by allowing the minimal humanitarian aid that has been permitted into Gaza.
The Yisrael Beiteinu leader has been a consistent supporter of Israel’s genocide and subsequent military actions. In September 2025, he joined other Israeli politicians in criticising Netanyahu for the apology to Doha that he had been forced to make by the Trump administration for that month’s strike, saying the apology was instead owed to the Israeli public. Equally, he has argued against any restraint in its invasion of Lebanon, telling the Jerusalem Post in March: “If once again we go for a limited operation, some manoeuvre near the border, maybe talk about a security zone, it’s complete madness.”
“Lieberman does not belong in this bloc,” said Shaiel Ben-Ephraim, an Israeli analyst with Atlas Global Strategies, arguing that his main motivation was hatred of Netanyahu.
However, Ben-Ephraim said, Lieberman “maintains a loyal base and is a crucial force in Israeli politics”. His core constituency is the former Soviet electorate, but he has gained wider trust through his perceived independence.
Observers say he could potentially return to Netanyahu’s right-wing bloc.
Yair Golan, the leader of ‘The Democrats’ a left-wing political alliance, is interviewed by Reuters in his office in Tel Aviv [Eloisa Lopez/Reuters] Yair Golan – Democrats
The only leader at Lapid’s table from the Zionist left and the only one open to working with Palestinian parties, Golan heads the Democrats, a liberal party that was formed in 2024 through the merger of Labor and the social democratic, secular Meretz.
A retired major-general and former deputy chief of staff, Golan, 64, earned domestic plaudits for driving south during the 2023 Hamas-led attack and helping repel it.
Golan is alone among many in the anti-Netanyahu bloc in his criticism of the government’s war aims, arguing that military achievements are being squandered without a coherent diplomatic and political strategy
In May 2025, two years into the genocide in Gaza, Golan sparked controversy when he told a news conference that a “ sane country does not kill babies as a hobby ”, accusing ministers of celebrating the starvation of Palestinian children in the territory. He later walked back the comments after widespread criticism in Israel. In October, while praising the achievements of the previous year’s 12-day war against Iran, he told party members that the assassination of Iranian Supreme Leader Ali Khamenei at the beginning of the conflict was a “mistake”. “Not because he didn’t deserve to die,” he caveated, “only because it doesn’t serve the interests of the State of Israel.”
Despite his openness to working with Palestinian parties, in September, Golan backed the disqualification of Sami Abu Shehadeh, leader of the Arab Balad party, over an article about October 7, drawing criticism from the Joint List – an alliance of the Arab-led Hadash, Ta’al and Balad parties – and commentators.
Roi Zur, a University of Essex specialist in Israeli politics, said that despite Golan and Lieberman’s ideological differences on security and conflict, there will be “no winning coalition without these two”. He added that concerns about a clash between them miss the point, noting that both oppose Netanyahu, favour secularisation and strongly oppose exemptions from military service for ultra-Orthodox Jews.

### 47. ★★★☆☆ Not cool: who hacked France’s supermarket fridges?

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-17T04:00:20+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Whether it was youngsters proving their skills or state sabotage, Europe’s most hacked country was left with a mountain of waste after food began cooking in freezersShortly before midnight last Sunday, the digital thermostats controlling hundreds of supermarket cold aisles across France began to flicker and rise.In a “bizarre and unprece

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Packages of perished goods piled outside an Intermarché store in Paris the day after a cyber-attack targeted refrigeration and freezer systems. Photograph: Lucien Libert/Reuters
Packages of perished goods piled outside an Intermarché store in Paris the day after a cyber-attack targeted refrigeration and freezer systems. Photograph: Lucien Libert/Reuters
Whether it was youngsters proving their skills or state sabotage, Europe’s most hacked country was left with a mountain of waste after food began cooking in freezers
Shortly before midnight last Sunday, the digital thermostats controlling hundreds of supermarket cold aisles across France began to flicker and rise.
In a “bizarre and unprecedented” cyber-attack, hackers had remotely overridden the temperature settings on thousands of freezers, fridges and chiller cabinets pushing them from as low as -27C (-16.6F) to well over 50C (122F).
Over the next four to six hours, the food inside first defrosted, then slowly began to cook.
Shop staff arriving early to restock shelves reported catching a blast of hot air, like opening an oven, when they went to fill freezers. One Normandy supermarket reported a number of its freezers had been pushed to 80C (176F). In certain stores, the extreme temperature rise had sparked fires triggering sprinklers and water damage.
“It was a catastrophe,” said one worker at the large Intermarché supermarket in the south-west suburb of Issy-les-Moulineaux in Paris . “They pushed the freezers here up to 62C. Everything had to be thrown away. It was such a waste.”
Supermarket staff had to remove tons of spoiled food and work late to reorder and restock shelves. Photograph: Lucien Libert/Reuters After removing tons of spoiled food, staff at the store worked until almost midnight on Monday to reorder and restock shelves. “Thankfully, we were able to get things almost back to normal the same day,” another shop worker added. “But we had to clear out everything in the freezers.”
If shop managers first assumed it was an isolated failure, it soon became clear up to 700 stores – including those from the Intermarché, Coopérative U, Carrefour, Leclerc and Monoprix chains along with a number of smaller independent shops – had been victims of a cyber-attack.
Dominique Schelcher, the president of Coopérative U, said 80 of its 2,000 supermarkets had been targeted. “This is a completely unprecedented, unexpected, and extremely serious attack. In my entire professional career, I’ve never seen anything like it,” the shocked executive told French radio.
The Paris public prosecutor’s office immediately opened an investigation into the latest in a series of cyber-attacks that have made France the most hacked country in Europe .
Gérôme Billois , a partner in the cybersecurity and technology company Wavestone, said the hack had the hallmarks of political sabotage.
“The two most feasible scenarios are either it was a group of youngsters or activists who wanted to blow up a few freezers to prove what they could do, or it was a destabilising attack, what we call sabotage, by internal or external state actors,” he said.
A woman walks past empty refrigerators at an Intermarché supermarket in Névez, western France. Photograph: Fred Tanneau/AFP/Getty “As for the attack’s signature, the most likely scenario is still a destabilisation attack – one designed to convey the message to populations that they are not safe, to convey the impression that the government is falling apart, and ultimately to instil fear.
“Currently there are two international actors known for doing this sort of thing: Iran and Russia.”
The hackers had pirated the controllers supplied by the Danish company Danfoss that controls the freezers and fridges. Most supermarket cold aisles are connected to the internet to allow remote maintenance and alerts for system failures. Initial investigations suggest the hackers disabled alarm systems before tampering with the thermostats, or extended the defrost programme by several hours to avoid triggering alarms.
Danfoss said it had identified security flaws last year and had “long recommended” that customers install updates or replace obsolete unsupported systems. The affected refrigeration units were “running outdated firmware”, it said. In the days following the first wave of attacks, France’s cyber-attack alert centre ANSSI confirmed supermarkets using controllers from two other companies were also targeted.
Marta Rybczyńska, a cybersecurity specialist and researcher, said she had found previous security warnings about old supermarket freezers running out-of-date software and newer models not having firmware updated.
French supermarket director apologises after cyber-attack spoils fresh and frozen food - video
“Some of these freezers hadn’t been supported by the manufacturers for several years and there were notices saying they should be replaced. Other freezers were newer but the software hadn’t been updated,” Rybczyńska said.
“The hackers found these weaknesses and used them.”
She added: “Many of the freezers appear to have been running with a default password. The hackers were able to modify or turn off the alerts so the shops only realised when the first person arrived in the morning and saw the damage that had been done.”
The most recent French cyber-attack mirrors a similar incident in the US in August when connected fridges and freezers at a dozen military commissaries – on-base food stores – went into unscheduled defrost cycles overnight causing massive food loss.
France is the most heavily pirated country in Europe by the volume of records exposed by hackers, according to the cybersecurity and risk management company Upguard.
In the last three years, hackers have breached France’s health system, France Travail, the government-run jobseekers agency, the National Agency for Identity Documents (ANTS) and most of the big mobile phone companies. This summer, the economy and finance ministry lost the personal and fiscal records of 678,000 taxpayers to a series of cyber-attacks.
Supermarket employees restock goods in Nancy, north-east France. Photograph: Muhammed Said Tako/Anadolu/Getty Billois says France has fallen behind in addressing cybersecurity and has still not adopted the EU’s Cyber Resilience Act (2024) that introduced mandatory security for internet-connected devices.
“It’s a text that’s not political and that everyone agrees on but is delayed because there’s always more urgent legislation or there are quibbles about amendments, and this has been a disaster,” he said.
In the UK, the cyber security and resilience (network and information systems) bill to tackle cyber threats, is wending a similarly tortuous way through parliament. However, it fails to directly cover the food industry.
Supermarket boss Schelcher was quick to shift the blame for the attack on to unnamed refrigeration contractors who maintain the cold aisle equipment. “Our supermarkets are not customers of Danfoss,” he told RTL radio.
As yet, no individual or group has claimed responsibility for the supermarket cyber-attack; Paris prosecutors have instructed detectives from its anti-cybercrime unit but Billois says finding the hackers will take time.
“It’s possible they’ll be identified eventually, but it could take weeks if not months to be certain because hackers know how to hide their tracks,” Billois said.
The Guardian view on food security: we have just-in-time, we need just-in-case | Editorial
Rybczyńska added: “Whether it’s possible to trace the hackers depends on their level of capability; if it’s a handful of youngsters, it’s easier because they probably won’t have thought to remove traces. If they are professionals it’s more complicated and will take longer. If they’re in a foreign country, it’ll be extremely difficult.”
The total cost of the cyber-attack has yet to be calculated but Thierry Cotillard, the CEO of Intermarché, said losses could run to “tens of millions of euros”, which store companies say is unlikely to be covered by their insurance.
“It’s a bizarre story,” Billois added. “There’s a shared responsibility between the supermarkets, the refrigeration companies who are sub-contractors and who maintain the material, and the software company. We cannot say who is to blame except, of course, the hackers.
“But in the end it comes down to freezers connected to the internet without being adequately secured. And that must be a wake-up call to everyone.”

### 48. ★★★☆☆ The Victory of Asymmetry in the Iran War - Council on Foreign Relations

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-16T16:56:08+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The Victory of Asymmetry in the Iran War&nbsp;&nbsp;Council on Foreign Relations

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

In the day-to-day of the Iran war, it’s natural to feel a touch of whiplash. Are we on the verge of agreement or at the dawn of escalation? Is Iran on the edge of collapse, or is it expanding the conflict? Will the Strait of Hormuz return to the status quo ante, or will tolling and periodic attacks on shipping be the new normal? These are important issues but there are larger, more fundamental strategic shifts underway as well. Not since 9/11 and the U.S. invasion of Iraq has the Middle East been in this degree of strategic flux.
It’s been nearly eight months since the war started, two hundred and thirty days to be precise, and, checking the tactical as well as the strategic report card, the record is decidedly mixed.
At the start of the conflict, the Trump administration sought to lay out its objectives. As Undersecretary of War for Policy Elbridge Colby put it at an event at the Council within a week of the war’s start: “The president has directed the United States Armed Forces to conduct a military campaign with a focus on degrading and destroying the Islamic Republic of Iran’s ability to project military power in the region, and potentially beyond.” Secretary of State Marco Rubio specified the objectives in even greater detail: “Number one, the destruction of their air force. Number two, the destruction of their navy. Number three, the severe diminishing of their missile launching capability. And number four, the destruction of their factories so they can’t make more missiles and more drones to threaten us in the future.”
On the basis of these goals, the war has achieved much of what it set out to do. By any measure, Iran has fewer military capabilities and a more strained economy than it had before. Iran’s navy sits at the bottom of the ocean, as the Trump administration likes to say, and its missile capabilities have been significantly degraded.
These results, of course, have not come without cost. Tragically, the lives of twenty-one U.S. service members have been lost, as well as over a thousand Iranian civilians. Additionally, the United States has spent down important munitions needed for its other key strategic commitments and priorities. By depleting Patriot interceptors and THAAD batteries, the United States faces increased challenges to maintain its force posture elsewhere around the world including, crucially, in the Indo-Pacific.
More broadly, though, if the goal of Operation Epic Fury was to prevent Iran from projecting power beyond its borders, it might well have had the opposite effect.
The most significant outcome of the war may well prove to be the power of asymmetry. Tehran knew when the war began that it lacked the direct firepower to defeat the United States, but it discovered that, with relatively few missiles, drones, and mines, it could turn its greatest asset—control over the Strait of Hormuz—into a powerful economic weapon that allows it to project power, at least for now, across the global economy.
The resulting repercussions on energy prices, inflation, and affordability in the United States led U.S. President Donald Trump to strike an agreement last week with Russian President Vladimir Putin to allow the sale of millions of barrels of Russian diesel. That sale, protested by Europeans and Ukrainians alike, is pouring more money into the coffers of the Russian war machine and has opened a U.S. rift with Europe and Ukraine, further complicating any path to ending that destructive and intractable war.
The long arm of Iran is also being felt in the country fields of England. Amid fears of a possible state-sponsored terrorist attack on the RAF Fairford base, the United States removed its long-range B–1 bombers, which played a key role in the war in Iran. Iran had previously attempted to reach out beyond its borders to assassinate dissidents as well as foreign officials involved in the 2020 attack on Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps General Qasem Soleimani, but a potential attack on a military base of a NATO ally may be one of the most striking examples of Iran’s sense of confidence despite the hits it has taken since February.
Back in Iran’s backyard, this week has also seen a significant escalation in the war between Saudi Arabia and the Houthis in Yemen. Just on Saturday, Houthi attacks on the international airport in Riyadh killed more than a dozen people and injured over three hundred more. This exchange of rockets is coming at a particularly precarious time for Saudi Arabia as a model of safety and stability in the region. On Monday, senior global energy executives from Chevron, ExxonMobil, Shell, BP, and TotalEnergies canceled their travel to Riyadh where they were scheduled to attend the WPC Energy Congress, a major energy conference.
The kingdom is set to host another big global event in ten days: its annual Future Investment Initiative conference, dubbed “Davos in the Desert.” This gathering of major capital allocators and heads of state is a crucial component of Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman’s high-profile, long-term modernization plan to shift the economy away from over-dependence on energy exports and toward technology, tourism, and entertainment. On the current trajectory, it would not surprise me if flights into Riyadh, assuming the airport is open, are half-empty at best.
To think that the poorly funded but scrappy Houthis are able to upset a multi-trillion-dollar, multi-decade strategy of one of the richest countries in the world is another example of the might of asymmetry. But it is the potential impact of this conflict on the United States’s regional strategy that could loom much larger.
For a long time, Iran has wanted to push the United States out of the region. Over the years, though, the United States increased its presence across thirteen bases in partner countries, including Bahrain, Kuwait, and Qatar, in order to deter aggression, promote stability, and preposition assets for conflicts as they arise.
As CFR’s Steven Cook has noted, Iran has turned the logic of those bases on its head. Partner countries initially sought U.S. bases as a source of security; now they are targets because of them. They assumed that the United States would actively defend them should Iran or its proxies attack; now that is open to question. After Iranian strikes damaged these bases, the United States withdrew certain forces, even as it deployed more mobile capabilities as part of a third carrier strike group and an additional Marine Expeditionary Unit. If and when the dust of this conflict settles, will partner countries want to rebuild those bases and have U.S. forces return to them? The long and drawn-out process of winding down the costly wars the United States has fought in the region for more than three decades will almost certainly be accelerated by this one.
And who or what would take the United States’ place? A drawdown of U.S. forces in the region would likely set off a frantic realignment of Middle Eastern politics and alliance networks. Without the U.S. security umbrella that so many of our regional partners thought they could depend on, they may turn to others, and each other, for that security. In the best case, that looks like a Middle Eastern NATO: an alliance or perhaps a lattice of alliances of like-minded neighbors committed to their collective security.
The recent Mecca Joint Defense Agreement between Pakistan, Saudi Arabia, and Turkey transforming into a more operational grouping is one possibility; another is the Abraham Accords, which tied Bahrain, Israel, Morocco, and the United Arab Emirates (UAE) closer together. And how will those countries seek to guarantee their security? Potentially through the pursuit of nuclear weapons. Given that the purpose of the initial strikes against Iran was to prevent nuclear proliferation in this region, it’s ironic that we may end up seeing additional countries in the region, including Saudi Arabia and the UAE, ultimately pursuing nuclear weapons capabilities.
And what is to come for Iran in this brave new world? Tehran today is weaker than it’s ever been and stronger than it almost certainly expected to be—both at the same time. How do you negotiate with a regime that is keenly aware of that? There is reporting that the United States might be planning for an escalation of attacks and that the Iranian regime is increasingly fragile. That said, the regime has proven more resilient than many expected, as has the Iranian economy, or at least the capacity of the Iranian people to absorb economic pain. This regime could well be a long-term participant in the future of Middle Eastern geopolitics, one that we might all have to learn to live with.
Let me know what you think about the balance of power in the Middle East and what this column should cover next by replying to [email&#160;protected] .
This work represents the views solely of the author(s). The Council on Foreign Relations is an independent, nonpartisan membership organization, think tank, and publisher, and takes no institutional positions on matters of policy.

Vance suggests disagreement with Trump over Iran war
Vance suggests disagreement with Trump over Iran war
‘Even if you disagree with him on that one issue, you can have that disagreement. And I think I have had that disagreement,’ US vice president says
HAMILTON, Canada US Vice President JD Vance suggested in an interview aired Friday that he may have disagreed with President Donald Trump over the Iran war.
“Even if you disagree with him on that one issue, you can have that disagreement. And I think I have had that disagreement,” Vance said on a political talk show hosted by former White House spokesman Sean Spicer.
Vance was responding to a question about his relationship with political commentator and former Trump ally Tucker Carlson, who has sharply criticized the Iran war, arguing that it serves Israeli rather than American interests and contradicts Trump’s pledge to avoid costly foreign military interventions.
Vance said that Carlson, who has espoused conservative views, was “very, very angry” over the war. “He and I have had that conversation in private, in public. I know how he feels, and I understand,” he said.
“What I've said to Tucker is don't throw the baby out with the bathwater … Aren't closed borders and ending the ridiculous anti-white racial discrimination in our university admissions, aren't these things worth actually acknowledging that Donald Trump has done a lot of good?”

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
